\noindent{\color{MidnightBlue}\large PROVEIT RESEARCH REPORT}\par
\vspace{0.06in}
\noindent{\Huge\bfseries Collision Geometry Is Linear}\par
\vspace{0.07in}
\noindent{\LARGE Diophantine Certificates for Rational Signal Machines\\[0.15em] and Conservative Particle Dynamics}\par
\vspace{0.07in}
\noindent{\large Event-sparse chambers, canonical quadratic and quartic certificates,\\ two routes to complete chronology, a conservative universal frontend,\\ sparse lattice certificates, conserved-mass thresholds, and mass four in every dimension}\par
\vspace{0.08in}
\noindent AI-assisted research report prepared for Vladimir Reshetnikov from twelve manuscripts\par
\noindent(ProveIt's incoming reports: batch~78, manuscripts~07 and~11; batch~79, manuscripts~16 and~19;\\ batch~80, manuscripts~10, 02, 05 and~03; batch~82, manuscripts~19, 06, 17 and~01), 2 October 2026\par
\vspace{0.08in}
\begin{abstract}\footnotesize\setlength{\parskip}{2pt}
A rational signal machine computes by the collisions of finitely many kinds of particles moving at fixed rational speeds on a line. Its first two Parts print two independent manuscripts that prove one theorem by two routes: once a complete finite collision history is fixed, its realizations are exactly the natural zeros of a sum of squares of integer affine residuals, of degree two, with exactly one witness tuple for each realization. In both, typed endpoint conditions on maximal adjacency intervals exclude unrecorded earlier collisions, omitted simultaneous inputs and omitted simultaneous sites, and causal depth controls the denominators of the event coordinates.

Part~I (source~07) eliminates every event coordinate. The realizing initial-gap vectors form a relatively open rational polyhedral cone cut out by at most $3(n-1)+(3m+1)E$ linear conditions, and the only witnesses are the slacks of the strict rows. The chamber geometry yields small integral realizations, an NP upper bound for bounded synthesis, a robustness--codimension criterion, a canonical first-hit quartic for a fixed event budget, exact quasi-polynomial seed counts, an exactly solved first-collision tournament and a four-speed accumulating clock with exponentially growing denominators. Part~II (source~11) keeps batch times and event positions as natural witnesses on a fixed rational grid, adds halting semantics, proves that the witness matrix has full column rank (so the certificate is strictly convex in its witnesses), gives linear witness and residual ledgers and a full-layer reference construction, and bounds the canonical witnesses by $60^K\max(1,M_0)$ for the four-speed interface of Durand-Lose's universal machine.

Parts~III and~IV (sources~12 and~13, from batch~79) certify conservative dynamics at a fixed horizon. Part~III writes out a reversible number-conserving universal signal machine (Morita's universal Turing machine through Durand-Lose's stacks) with 18 live signals, whose event dynamics is an 18-dimensional integer-linear map with a canonical quadratic step certificate; Part~IV compiles one-dimensional number-conserving lattice dynamics into a quartic sum of squares with a unique natural witness and proves decidability at total mass at most two.

Parts~V and~VI (sources~14--17, batch~80) find the least conserved mass of an undecidable observation. With many weight-one symbols, a fixed reversible automaton simulates a universal reversible two-counter machine at total mass three, also for exact pairs (Part~V); with at most one, masses three and four stay decidable, so integer-state number-conserving automata need five particles (Part~VI). Source~18 and Part~VII (sources~19--21, batch~82) add timed quartics and mass-four decidability on every $\Z^d$.

None of the seven Parts compiles unbounded halting into one fixed-arity polynomial or improves the repository's 75- and 87-operation bounds; Parts~I and~II do not transcribe the published universal machine, and Part~III transcribes a different one. Re-derived devices are printed as pointers or second routes. Part~I's convex-quadratic barrier is a special case of Part~XI of the collection's report \emph{canonical-diophantine-certificates}. The report is AI-assisted and unrefereed; nothing in it is formalized in Lean or Rocq.
\end{abstract}
\vfill
\noindent\small\textbf{Status.} Conventional proofs and finite exact-arithmetic checks; not kernel-checked. Section~\ref{smc:sec:front} compares the two routes, introduces Parts~III--VII, and states what is not claimed; Appendix~\ref{smc:app:provenance} gives the provenance.
\end{titlepage}
\hypersetup{pageanchor=true}

\section{Scope, the two routes, and conventions}\label{smc:sec:front}

Sections~\ref{smc:cs:sec:batch79}--\ref{smc:cs:sec:questions} were added on 2 October 2026, when two manuscripts of batch~79 became Parts~\ref{smc:cs:part} and~\ref{smc:sl:part}; where they make a sentence of the earlier subsections stale, a dated note says so. Sections~\ref{smc:tm:sec:batch80}--\ref{smc:tm:sec:frontquestions} were added later the same day, when four manuscripts of batch~80 became Parts~\ref{smc:tm:part} and~\ref{smc:su:part}, again with dated notes where earlier sentences became stale. Sections~\ref{smc:zd:sec:batch82}--\ref{smc:zd:sec:frontquestions} were added on 3 October 2026, when four manuscripts of batch~82 became source~18 of Part~\ref{smc:su:part} and Part~\ref{smc:zd:part}, likewise with dated notes.

\subsection{The two sources}\label{smc:sec:parts}

This report is built from two AI-assisted research manuscripts that arrived in one delivery as batch~78 of ProveIt's incoming reports (manuscripts~07 and~11; Appendix~\ref{smc:app:provenance} gives the full provenance). They are called \emph{source~07} and \emph{source~11} after their batch-78 manuscript numbers, which are also the file prefixes of their shipped programs and data. Neither cites the other, and a text-overlap check at placement found almost no shared wording (at most 0.5 per cent of either text's eight-word sequences occur in the other). They nevertheless prove the same theorem, by the same mechanism, in two different witness formats (Section~\ref{smc:sec:routes}).

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Part~\ref{smc:part:cg} (source~07, the base), \emph{Collision geometry is linear}.} Arbitrary rational speeds, of either sign. Every event time and position is eliminated as a rational linear form in the initial gaps, so a complete chronological skeleton becomes a homogeneous rational chamber (Theorem~\ref{smc:cg:thm:chamber}) and a convex quadratic with unique slack witnesses (Theorem~\ref{smc:cg:thm:quadratic}). The chamber geometry then gives denominator and height bounds, small integral realizations, an NP upper bound, robustness and codimension, finite-horizon semilinearity, a canonical first-hit quartic, exact Ehrhart counts, two exact examples and a convex-quadratic barrier.
\item \textbf{Part~\ref{smc:part:sd} (source~11), \emph{Direct Diophantine certificates}.} Speeds and positions normalized to natural numbers by an external Galilean change of coordinates. Batch times and event positions stay in the certificate as natural witnesses on the grid $B=D^K$, and the certificate has prefix and halting versions (Theorem~\ref{smc:sd:thm:main}). Its additions are the halting semantics with terminal rays, full column rank of the witness matrix and hence strict convexity in the witnesses (Lemma~\ref{smc:sd:lem:rank}), an explicit witness and residual ledger, a full-layer reference construction with exact counts (Section~\ref{smc:sd:sec:fulllayer}), the witness-height bound for Durand-Lose's four-speed interface (Theorem~\ref{smc:sd:thm:height}) and a positive-witness translation.
\end{itemize}

Source~07 is the base because it assumes less (signed rational speeds, no normalization interface) and proves more about the chamber; source~11 is printed in full as the second route together with its additions. Each Part keeps its source's definitions, letters and numbering of statements. (\wtag\ Added 2 October 2026: two further Parts, from batch~79, follow Part~II; Section~\ref{smc:cs:sec:batch79} describes them. They do not prove the common theorem of this subsection and the next again.) (\wtag\ Added 2 October 2026, batch~80: two more Parts, from four manuscripts of batch~80, follow Part~IV, so that the report now prints eight sources in six Parts; Section~\ref{smc:tm:sec:batch80} describes them.) (\wtag\ Added 3 October 2026, batch~82: a third source joins Part~VI and a seventh Part follows it, so that the report now prints twelve sources in seven Parts; Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:batch82} describes them.) The common theorem is stated in both Parts, because its two statements have different inputs and witnesses; where a lemma of Part~II coincides with one of Part~I, a \wtag\ note at the lemma says so, and source~11's proof is kept as a marked second route.

\subsection{One theorem by two routes}\label{smc:sec:routes}

\textbf{The common theorem.} Fix a rational signal machine, an initial label word and a finite chronological collision history (a \emph{skeleton} in Part~I, a \emph{schema} in Part~II). There is an effective sum of squares of integer affine residuals, in the input coordinates and natural witnesses, whose natural zeros are exactly the realizations of that history, with exactly one witness tuple for each realization; its degree is at most two. This is Theorems~\ref{smc:cg:thm:chamber} and~\ref{smc:cg:thm:quadratic} of Part~I and Theorem~\ref{smc:sd:thm:main} of Part~II (for prefix semantics).

\textbf{The common mechanism.} Both sources record each pair of adjacent signals once, over the maximal interval during which it stays adjacent, and impose a \emph{typed} condition at each end of that interval: equality exactly when both segments are born (or die) at the same collision there, and strict positivity otherwise, including for a pair that survives the last listed batch. Affineness of the gap then excludes every interior meeting (Part~I, Lemma~\ref{smc:cg:lem:endpoint}; Part~II, Lemma~\ref{smc:sd:lem:interior}), and the strict conditions at a batch boundary make the declared blocks exactly the collision sites (the soundness part of the proof of Theorem~\ref{smc:cg:thm:chamber}; Lemma~\ref{smc:sd:lem:boundary}). The two sources then prove the same auxiliary statements: the lifetime count $(n-1)+\sum_e(q_e+1)$ (\eqref{smc:cg:eq:Jbound} and \eqref{smc:sd:eq:Lbound}, where Part~II writes $|\out(e)|$ for $q_e$), causal-depth denominators dividing $D^h$ (Theorem~\ref{smc:cg:thm:denominator}, Lemma~\ref{smc:sd:lem:denom}), and semilinearity of the inputs accepted under a fixed event bound (Theorem~\ref{smc:cg:thm:semilinear}, Proposition~\ref{smc:sd:prop:semilinear}). Table~\ref{smc:tab:routes} compares the two formats.

\textbf{Normalizations.} The two definitions of $D$ agree. Part~I multiplies all speeds by an integer $L$ and leaves their signs; Part~II subtracts a common speed $c$ and multiplies by an integer $m$. A common shift does not change speed differences, so with $m=L$ both take $D$ to be the least common multiple of the same nonzero integer differences. Part~II needs nonnegative speeds and an origin because its inputs are natural absolute positions; Part~I needs neither because its input is the gap vector. Part~I's integer seeds come from homogeneity (scaling a rational chamber point); Part~II fixes the scale outside the witness tuple and warns that a scale witness or variable denominators would not preserve its canonical quadratic claim without a new argument (Section~\ref{smc:sd:sec:normalize}).

\textbf{Two shuttles, one mechanism.} Part~I's clock and Part~II's shuttle are both a particle reflected between two converging walls, each round trip shrinking the remaining time to the accumulation by a factor three, so that the reduced denominators grow exponentially in the number of events while their bit lengths grow linearly. They are different machines: Part~I's has a stationary left wall and a right wall of speed $-1$, and its every finite prefix has the whole positive quadrant as chamber (robust chronology with exponentially growing precision); Part~II's lives entirely in $0<t<2$, $0<x<6$ and is stated for its natural-speed interface. Neither source gives the accumulation point a continuation. Source~07 credits the accumulation phenomenon to Becker et al.~\cite{accumulations}.

\textbf{Two missed simultaneities, one failure mode.} In both examples a proposed batch omits a second, spatially separate collision that happens at the same time, and in both the omitted pair is an unchanged adjacent pair whose gap reaches zero exactly at the last listed boundary. The strict upper endpoint of a surviving pair rejects it: in Part~I through the chamber row that must be positive there, in Part~II through the residual $G(T_1)-h^+-1=-h^+-1$. Part~II adds the observation that weaker conditions (two nonnegative endpoint gaps with positive sum) would accept the false schema.

\textbf{Two meanings of ``sparse''.} In Part~I ``event-sparse'' counts geometric conditions: the bound \eqref{smc:cg:eq:Rbound} is linear in $n$ and $E$, but a propagated intercept may depend on every initial gap, so the rows can be dense and storing them can take $O(dR_\sigma)$ coefficients (the paragraph after Theorem~\ref{smc:cg:thm:rows}). In Part~II the sparse certificate also has residuals of bounded width, because event coordinates are witnesses; its coefficients then contain the grid constant $B=D^K$. \emph{Neither} size statement is a constant arithmetic-operation count for an unbounded computation, as both sources say.

\subsection{Notions and letters}\label{smc:sec:notation}

Each Part keeps its source's letters. Many letters carry different meanings in the two Parts; Table~\ref{smc:tab:notation} lists every letter used in both, so that a statement of one Part is never read with the other Part's meaning. Inside a Part the meaning is always the one in that Part's column. No symbol was renamed and no normalization was changed: source~11's macro for the natural numbers is called \verb|\NN| in this file and still prints $\NN$, and Part~I prints $\N$; both denote $\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$. (\wtag\ The letters of Parts~III and~IV are listed in Section~\ref{smc:cs:sec:notation}, and those of Parts~V and~VI in Section~\ref{smc:tm:sec:notation}.)

Three words need the same care.
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Skeleton} (Part~I) and \textbf{schema} (Part~II) carry the same information: an ordered list of batches, each a set of disjoint contiguous blocks of the live list. A skeleton lists only the collision blocks (at least two members); a schema partitions the whole live list and marks singletons as persisting signals. A \emph{site} of Part~I is an \emph{event} of Part~II.
\item \textbf{Realization.} Part~I's realization and Part~II's prefix realization are the same notion: exactly the listed batches as the first batches, nothing asserted afterwards. Part~II's \emph{halting} realization additionally excludes every future collision. The ``halting relation'' of Part~I's Section~\ref{smc:cg:sec:barrier} is the undecidable halting of a universal machine, a different thing.
\item \textbf{Convex.} Part~I's certificate is convex (positive semidefinite Hessian) jointly in seed and slacks; Part~II's is \emph{strictly} convex in the witnesses with the input held fixed, and Part~II states that joint strict convexity is neither needed nor generally true.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Relation to the repository and to the literature}\label{smc:sec:relation}

Neither source cites the related material below; this subsection supplies the cross-references (pins and dates in Appendix~\ref{smc:app:provenance}). Reports of the collection are cited by label name, since their labels do not resolve in this file.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Report \emph{canonical-diophantine-certificates}, Part~XI}~\cite{cdc}, ``Order-free self-assembly and a positivity-restricted degree threshold''. Its Lemma \texttt{cdc:of:lem:affinezero}, Theorem \texttt{cdc:of:thm:cubicsemilinear} and Theorem \texttt{cdc:of:thm:classification} ($\mathrm D^+_2(k)=\mathrm D^+_3(k)=\mathrm{SL}(k)$) prove, for polynomials of degree at most three that are nonnegative only on the real nonnegative orthant, that the full natural zero set is a finite union of natural solution sets of rational affine systems, hence semilinear and effectively so. Part~I's Theorem~\ref{smc:cg:thm:rigidity} and Corollary~\ref{smc:cg:cor:barrier} are the globally nonnegative quadratic special case; they are printed with source~07's direct proof, as a second route, and credited in Remark~\ref{smc:cg:rem:credit}. No novelty is claimed for them. The same Part's Theorem \texttt{cdc:of:thm:singlefoldsl} (a single-fold orthant-nonnegative quadratic for every semilinear set) bears on Part~I's first-hit quartic; see Remark~\ref{smc:cg:rem:singlefold}. The bounded-search argument at the end of Part~I's Section~\ref{smc:cg:sec:barrier} is the standard one of Proposition \texttt{cdc:bd:prop:nobound} there, for a different statement (site counts of accepting runs).
\item \textbf{Report \emph{liveness-beyond-halting}}~\cite{lbh}. Its Part~V prints a question of its source~11, ``Optimal degree under stronger geometric restrictions'', which asks for the minimal degree and dimension of a witness-free, binary-branching local polynomial that is globally nonnegative on all real inputs, and remarks that the globally nonnegative quadratic case ``has a much more rigid affine geometry''. Part~I's Theorem~\ref{smc:cg:thm:rigidity} (and, more generally, Part~XI of~\cite{cdc}) states that geometry: such a quadratic has a rational affine zero set. This report does not settle that degree question.
\item \textbf{The Hilbert-tenth-problem programme has already reviewed both manuscripts.} The review of the delivery of 2 October 2026~\cite{review808}, with its detailed signal-machine part~\cite{reviewsignal}, replayed both suites from the original archives, read both articles in full and reports ``PASS for both reports' stated finite-schema, exact-domain theorems and generated examples'', with no mandatory correction. It also proves and checks two reductions that neither source claims: deleting source~11's identically zero same-birth rows and its same-death rows (differences of retained flight rows), and erasing the initial-order slacks when $K>0$ or the certificate is halting, which takes the worked annihilation from six residuals and five witnesses to four and four (a bijection of natural zero sets, not of nonnegative rational ones, with a rational counterexample); and deleting redundant strict rows, with computed slacks, from the five exported packets of source~07. Those reductions are the review's, are not re-proved here, and do not change any theorem of either Part. This report's intake is therefore not a first review; it adds the merge, the cross-references and the credit of Remark~\ref{smc:cg:rem:credit}.
\item \textbf{The programme's audits of finite-history models.} Source~07 cites the reversible finite-history audit~\cite{reversible} for its distinction between cheap local relations and a complete computation certificate; that file is unchanged from source~07's pin to the write. A sibling audit~\cite{blockca} notes that ``finite particles can in principle store unbounded values in their separation'' and that ``a new construction would have to establish that interface''. Durand-Lose's halting universal signal machine~\cite{durandlose} uses a finite initial configuration and a halting event (Part~II, Section~\ref{smc:sd:sec:purpose}), so signal machines are a candidate for such an interface, and both Parts compile its finite collision histories in principle; but neither transcribes its universal rule table or its input loader (Part~I, Section~\ref{smc:cg:sec:implementation}; Part~II, Question~\ref{smc:sd:q:universal}), so this report does not establish that interface. (\wtag\ Added 2 October 2026: for signal machines Part~III now establishes it, with a different universal machine and a prepared rational loader; see Section~\ref{smc:cs:sec:relation}.)
\item \textbf{The repository's universal bounds.} The project README~\cite{repo} still states the established bounds of 75 operations for a complete certificate and 87 for one universal polynomial. Neither Part improves or bears on them; both say so. (\wtag\ Nor do Parts~III--VI.)
\item \textbf{Other reports of the category.} No result of \emph{probabilistic-quantum-and-continuous-computation} or \emph{group-theoretic-substrates} is re-proved here. Apart from the programme's review described above, nothing in the repository treated signal machines before this report.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Status and what is not claimed}\label{smc:sec:status}

The report is AI-assisted and unrefereed, and \textbf{nothing in it is formalized in Lean or Rocq}. Both sources give conventional proofs together with finite exact-arithmetic checks that are regression evidence, not proofs. Every limitation stated by either source is kept in its Part; collected here, neither Part claims
\begin{itemize}
\item historical priority, or exhaustive novelty, for its constructions (Part~I, Section~\ref{smc:cg:sec:intro}; Part~II, Section~\ref{smc:sd:sec:purpose}); Part~II adds that a general linearizability of signal-machine evolution is already in Durand-Lose's linear real-register simulation~\cite{DL07};
\item one fixed-arity polynomial for unbounded halting, a single-fold or finite-fold Diophantine representation of c.e.\ sets, or a fixed-universal arithmetic-operation bound: the skeleton or schema, the machine and the input length are compiler data, and the row and residual counts are not operation counts;
\item that the published universal machine (13 meta-signals, 21 non-blank rules) or its input loader has been transcribed, executed or certified;
\item any semantics at or after an accumulation point; the clock and the shuttle are tests of finite prefixes, and the clock is not a claim to have discovered accumulation;
\item NP-hardness (Corollary~\ref{smc:cg:cor:NP} is an upper bound), an efficient enumeration of first-hit chambers or of their volumes, or an optimized exhaustive enumerator in the code;
\item that the denominator-growth examples are lower bounds for arbitrary Diophantine representations: Part~II's is a lower bound for common-grid encodings;
\item that integer scaling of a rational seed preserves a prescribed physical length or deadline;
\item that the finite checks are exhaustive, or that the shared simulator of Part~II's two compilers is independently verified;
\item any Lean or Rocq verification; neither source's code is a verified compiler.
\end{itemize}
The report itself claims no novelty for Part~I's convex-quadratic barrier (Remark~\ref{smc:cg:rem:credit}) and none for the reductions of the programme's review. (\wtag\ The non-claims of Parts~III and~IV are collected in Section~\ref{smc:cs:sec:status}, those of Parts~V and~VI in Section~\ref{smc:tm:sec:status}, and those of source~18 and Part~VII in Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:status}.)

Four of Part~II's questions coincide with, or overlap, directions of Part~I; Table~\ref{smc:tab:questions} pairs them, so that the two formulations are read together. None of them is answered by the other source, by the repository, or by the programme's review; the one partial overlap is that Part~II's Question~\ref{smc:sd:q:ledger} begins with causal-depth clearing, which both sources already prove (a \wtag\ note there re-scopes it). \subsection{Parts III and IV: conservative frontends (batch 79)}\label{smc:cs:sec:batch79}

\wtag\ This subsection and the four that follow were written on 2 October 2026, when two manuscripts of batch~79 of ProveIt's incoming reports were added to the report as Parts~\ref{smc:cs:part} and~\ref{smc:sl:part}. They are called \emph{source~12} and \emph{source~13} after the file prefixes of their shipped programs and data, which continue this report's local sequence after~07 and~11. Their batch-79 manuscript numbers are different: \textbf{source~12 is batch-79 manuscript~16, and source~13 is batch-79 manuscript~19}. Appendix~\ref{smc:app:provenance} gives the full provenance. Neither manuscript cites Parts~I and~II, the other new manuscript, or any repository file.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Part~\ref{smc:cs:part} (source~12), \emph{Conservative signal machines as Diophantine frontends}.} A fixed reversible, number-conserving universal signal machine $\mathcal U_{18}$, written out completely: 114 meta-signals, 445 explicit injective two-to-two collision rules, speeds $\{-12,-9,-4,0,4,9,12\}$ and exactly 18 live signals on every encoded input (Theorem~\ref{smc:cs:thm:literal}). Its source is Morita's 15-state, 6-symbol reversible universal Turing machine, compiled through Durand-Lose's 2012 conservative rational stacks; the input of an explicitly halting cyclic-tag program is loaded into two rational distances. At event cuts the complete simultaneous-event dynamics of any fixed-population, equal-cardinality machine is a partial piecewise homogeneous integer-linear map with the pivot-scaled gap matrices $N_j=c_jI-ce_j^{\mathsf T}$, $N_j^2=c_jN_j$, plus one mode coordinate (Sections~\ref{smc:cs:sec:event}--\ref{smc:cs:sec:integer}). A selector/copy construction then gives one explicit quadratic polynomial with a unique natural witness for each legal step, and for each fixed horizon (Theorem~\ref{smc:cs:thm:packet}); for $\mathcal U_{18}$ the step polynomial has exactly 4,196,998 auxiliary variables over a closed family of 49,700 modes and 80,501 branches (Appendix~\ref{smc:cs:app:closure}). A ten-signal binary version is proved to exist but not printed (Theorem~\ref{smc:cs:thm:binary}).
\item \textbf{Part~\ref{smc:sl:part} (source~13), \emph{Sparse Diophantine certificates for finite mass lattice dynamics}.} The discrete counterpart: one-dimensional partitioned number-conserving cellular automata with three lanes. A conserved mass $M$ bounds the number of unit records but not the gaps between them, and a fixed horizon $T$ of any supplied total conservative table compiles into one quartic sum of squares with exactly one natural zero, paying for collision detection, simultaneous redistribution, sorting, table lookup and empty space, with exact witness and residual ledgers (Theorem~\ref{smc:sl:thm:core}) and an option that is exact on the nonnegative real orthant. A source interface to Morita and Imai's reversible conservative automaton corrects a printed sign, adds a reachable-legality hypothesis that an explicit false observer shows to be necessary, pays for a loader and a first-pulse observer, and works a fully specified, nonuniversal doubling example. Decidability results bound what any such frontend can do: under a bounded orbit diameter, and without it at total mass at most two (Theorem~\ref{smc:sl:thm:two}); at mass at most two the relation is effectively semilinear (Theorem~\ref{smc:sl:thm:semilinear}).
\end{itemize}

\textbf{How they relate to Parts~I and~II.} Neither new Part proves the common theorem of Section~\ref{smc:sec:routes} again; both certify a step, or a fixed horizon, of a \emph{deterministic} dynamics rather than the realizations of a prescribed collision history. Part~III meets Parts~I and~II in one lemma: its complete event selection (Lemma~\ref{smc:cs:lem:event}) is their typed-endpoint argument for a single batch, and its uniform lift is their common-denominator device (\wtag\ notes at both places). Part~III is also the instantiated universal frontend that Part~I's research direction~\ref{smc:cg:q:universal} asks for, though for a different universal machine than the one named there (Section~\ref{smc:cs:sec:questions}). Part~IV shares no lemma with Parts~I--III; it answers, for lattice dynamics, the conservative-population question of Part~II.

\textbf{Two Morita sources, no shared data.} Both new Parts draw their universal source from Morita, but from different constructions: Part~III from Morita's 2008 reversible universal Turing machine UTM(15,6), through Durand-Lose's signal-machine stacks; Part~IV from Morita and Imai's 2001 number-conserving reversible partitioned automaton, built on Morita's 1996 reversible two-counter machine. They share no table, program or data file. Part~III prints its universal machine in full; Part~IV deliberately leaves its safe universal source as an existence construction and prints only a nonuniversal example.

Inside a Part the meaning is always that Part's.

Several words need the same care.
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Horizon.} Part~III's horizon is $K$ steps of the event map, Part~IV's is $T$ lattice steps; Part~II's $K$ is a number of collision batches of a prescribed schema. In Parts~III and~IV the horizon is a compiler parameter that fixes the number of variables; it is never a free unknown of one polynomial.
\item \textbf{Batch.} Part~III's ``signal batches'' (Section~\ref{smc:cs:sec:replay}) are the collision batches of Parts~I--II, counted along a run. Its \emph{mode} is an ordered label word, and a \emph{germ} is a post-event state whose zero gaps separate.
\item \textbf{Source.} ``Source~12'' and ``source~13'' name manuscripts; inside Parts~III and~IV ``the source'' is the simulated machine (Morita's Turing machine, Morita's two-counter machine).
\item \textbf{Conservative.} Part~III's rules conserve the number of signals and a positive energy; Part~IV's local tables conserve the total lane mass. Both are reversible in the cases printed, but neither certificate theorem needs reversibility.
\item \textbf{Sparse.} Part~III's ``sparse scaled projections'' have at most $2(n-2)$ nonzero entries; Part~IV's ``sparse'' certificate keeps $M$ records however large the gaps between them. Neither is Part~I's event-sparse row count, and none of them is a fixed-arity statement.
\item \textbf{Loader.} Part~III's loader writes a prepared tape into two rational distances at a cost linear in the tape length (Section~\ref{smc:cs:sec:loader}); Part~IV's uniform loader leaves two counter positions as free natural parameters and pays $4+3M(M-1)$ witnesses and residuals \eqref{smc:sl:eq:loader}. Neither converts an ordinary binary integer input.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Relation of Parts III and IV to the repository}\label{smc:cs:sec:relation}

\wtag\ Neither new source cites the material below; these cross-references are added. Results of other reports are cited by label name.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Re-derived results, printed as pointers or second routes.} Part~III's complete event selection (Lemma~\ref{smc:cs:lem:event}) is the one-batch case of Part~I's Theorem~\ref{smc:cg:thm:chamber}, and its uniform lift is the denominator device of Theorem~\ref{smc:cg:thm:denominator} and Part~II's grid. Part~IV's comparison gadget is Lemma \texttt{cdc:mem:lem:compare} and its sorting comparator the two-field record comparator (\texttt{cdc:mem:prop:comparator-cost}) of Part~V of \emph{canonical-diophantine-certificates}~\cite{cdc}; Part~IV's horizon-free quartic (Corollary~\ref{smc:sl:cor:fixedarity}) is a degree-four second route to the single-fold degree-two representation of semilinear sets of that report's Part~XI (\texttt{cdc:of:thm:singlefoldsl}); and the finite-search step of Proposition~\ref{smc:sl:prop:cutoff} is \texttt{cdc:bd:prop:nobound}. Each place carries a \wtag\ note; no novelty is claimed for any of them.
\item \textbf{The degree-two orthant format.} Part~III's step polynomial is literally of the form $\sum A_i^2+\sum B_jC_j$ of the spine of \emph{quadratic-orthant-certificates} (\texttt{qoc:eq:spine}), nonnegative on the real orthant; its existence also follows from \texttt{cdc:of:thm:singlefoldsl}, since a step relation with finitely many linear branches is semilinear; source~12's contribution is the explicit construction and its ledger. Its selector/copy construction is Balas's disjunctive formulation~\cite{balas1979,balas1985,jeroslowlowe}, credited at the place. (Added 2 October 2026, batch~79: that report's Parts~V and~VI use the same selector device with the selector added to the right factor of each product, which makes their nonnegative real fibres natural; the note after Theorem~\ref{smc:cs:thm:packet} compares the two gates and shows that the real fibre of Part~III's packet is natural at every natural input $X\ne0$, and at $X=0$ as well under the theorem's disjoint homogeneous branch hypothesis; the weak gates alone do not force one-hot selection at zero.) Part~IV's certificates are sums of squares of quadratic residuals, of degree four; its orthant-exact option restores exactness on the nonnegative real orthant with norm-one residuals.
\item \textbf{The finite-input interface asked for by a programme audit.} The block-automaton audit~\cite{blockca} notes that finite particles could store unbounded values in their separations and that a new construction would have to establish that interface. For signal machines, Part~III establishes it: a fixed machine with 18 live signals, finite support, stack values stored in two distances, and observable halt and null outcomes with an escape of eight crossings. It does so with a prepared rational loader, not from an ordinary integer, and not for billiard-ball or block automata, which remain as the audit leaves them. (This supersedes, for signal machines, the last sentence of the corresponding item of Section~\ref{smc:sec:relation}.)
\item \textbf{Other reports.} Part~IV's mass-two theorem is related only loosely to Part~I's rigidity theorem (both end in semilinearity, for different reasons). Part~IV bears on the question ``Local certificates on adaptive support geometry'' of Part~XVI of \emph{canonical-diophantine-certificates} without answering it (note at Part~IV's questions). The fixed-horizon form of Part~III is the same kind of statement as the trace theorem \texttt{pqc:pm:thm:trace} of \emph{probabilistic-quantum-and-continuous-computation}, for different dynamics.
\item \textbf{The programme's reviews.} The Hilbert-tenth-problem programme reviewed the earlier edition of source~12 (batch-79 manuscript~07) and wrote the patch that the corrected edition printed here applies~\cite{reviewcs}, and then reviewed the corrected edition itself, with a natural-domain projection of its packet~\cite{reviewcsc}. It reviewed source~13 completely and wrote a constructor patch~\cite{reviewsl}, which was not applied here; the corrected code edition of source~13 (batch~80) contains an equivalent repair of its own and is now shipped, and the programme's correction audit~\cite{reviewbeighty} confirms it. It later reduced two of source~13's ledgers~\cite{reviewslproj,reviewcong}. Its review index~\cite{reviewaebfa} lists all of these as complete, and its placement audit~\cite{reviewplace} authenticates every file of both sources placed by \texttt{a7ae02511} against the delivered archives (five of source~13's have since been replaced by the corrected edition's bytes). Sections~\ref{smc:cs:sec:replay} and~\ref{smc:sl:sec:reproduction} give the details. No review changes a theorem of this report, and the reductions are the programme's, not re-proved here.
\item \textbf{The repository's universal bounds.} The 75- and 87-operation bounds stated in the project README~\cite{repo} are untouched by Parts~III and~IV, which say so themselves (no fixed-arity polynomial for unbounded histories). The programme's review of source~12's earlier edition records the same conclusion.
\item \textbf{Formal status.} Nothing in Parts~III and~IV is formalized in Lean or Rocq; the Hilbert-tenth-problem Lean development has no signal-machine, cellular-automaton or Presburger-compiler module that states any of their results. One classical ingredient that Part~IV imports is formalized elsewhere in the repository: Cooper's quantifier elimination for Presburger arithmetic, used in Section~\ref{smc:sl:sec:semilinearity}, is the Lean development \path{Logic/PresburgerArithmetic} (for instance \texttt{PresburgerArithmetic.Formula.holds\_iff\_quantifierEliminate} and the decision procedure \texttt{PresburgerArithmetic.Formula.presburgerArithmetic\_decidable}). That formalizes the imported step only, over the integers, and none of Part~IV's own statements.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Status and what Parts III and IV do not claim}\label{smc:cs:sec:status}

\wtag\ Both new sources give conventional proofs and finite exact-arithmetic checks, which are regression evidence, not proofs; both are AI-assisted and unrefereed. Every limitation they state is kept in its Part. Collected here, they do not claim
\begin{itemize}
\item priority, or an exhaustive literature search, for the precise synthesis (Part~III), the sparse constructions or the mass-two theorem (Part~IV); a new universal computational mechanism or new classical universality; the stack, selector/copy and circuit-to-sum-of-squares devices as new;
\item a fixed-arity polynomial for an unknown, unbounded horizon or for arbitrary halting histories; the horizon fixes the number of variables in both Parts, and ``there exists some finite horizon'' is an unbounded union that needs a separate compression theorem;
\item for Part~III: that the ten-signal binary interpreter is printed or instantiated; optimality of 18 or ten live signals, of the loader or of the dimension; injectivity of the integer event map; that every path of the 49,700-mode graph is realizable from one input (it is a mode-only overapproximation), or that the graph bounds the length of a universal run; a flat monomial expansion (the trillion-term count is derived, not emitted); a minimal gate count; any semantics for malformed rational configurations or at accumulation points; that the finite replays prove universality, which is imported from Morita's theorem; that the polynomial is convex or a sum of squares over all reals;
\item for Part~IV: a printed universal source table or its state count; faithful interpretation of arbitrary counter pairs; intrinsic universality, an efficient simulation, exact-target undecidability, or global halting (the pulse is an anchored observation); a minimum universal mass, or that three particles suffice; any statement over unrestricted reals; that the displayed counts are lower bounds; an arbitrary-rule quantifier eliminator; and anything about arbitrary c.e.\ sets from the decidable mass-two subclass (\wtag\ added 2 October 2026, batch~80: these remain true of Part~IV; Parts~V and~VI now prove exact-target undecidability and the sufficiency of three units for a weighted automaton with many weight-one symbols, and decidability at masses three and four with a single one; Section~\ref{smc:tm:sec:frontquestions});
\item a proof-assistant verification; neither source's code is a verified compiler.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Research questions answered or touched by Parts III and IV}\label{smc:cs:sec:questions}

\wtag\ Dated notes at the questions themselves record the following; Table~\ref{smc:tab:questions} carries the same information in its relation column.
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Part~I's direction~\ref{smc:cg:q:universal}} (an audited universal signal-machine frontend) is \emph{answered by Part~III, except for the ordinary-integer loader}: Part~III instantiates a published finite-input universal construction with its exact labels, speeds, all collision rules, forbidden (not transparent) undefined encounters, input encoding and marked acceptance, audits simultaneous and triple collisions, and proves the correspondence of its loader with the simulated machine. Two differences from the direction as worded remain. The machine is Morita's UTM(15,6) through Durand-Lose's 2012 stacks, not the 13-type construction of~\cite{durandlose} named as a candidate; and the input is a prepared cyclic-tag tape loaded as two rational distances, so the equivalence with ``an ordinary integer program input'' would still need a digit-extraction and base-conversion loader, whose cost Part~III leaves explicit (Section~\ref{smc:cs:sec:loader}).
\item \textbf{Part~II's Question~\ref{smc:sd:q:universal}} (transcribe the 13-label, 21-rule machine) stays open as stated: Part~III transcribes a different universal machine.
\item \textbf{Part~II's Question~\ref{smc:sd:q:conservative}} (exploit conservative bounded populations) is \emph{answered in part, at a fixed horizon}, by both new Parts. Part~III instantiates every finite transition branch of a reversible conservative universal signal machine, pays for strict guards and for its loader, and excludes accumulation on encoded runs; Part~IV does the same for lattice dynamics, where conserved mass bounds the records but not the gaps. Neither encodes unbounded histories in a fixed arity, which the question also mentions.
\item \textbf{Part~II's Questions~\ref{smc:sd:q:ledger} and~\ref{smc:sd:q:compress}} are not answered; Part~III's literal ledger is a variable-and-residual count, not the gate ledger asked for.
\item \textbf{Part~IV's own five questions} (Section~\ref{smc:sl:sec:questions}) are open; Part~III states no separate questions. (\wtag\ Added 2 October 2026, batch~80: the fourth and fifth are now answered, in substance and for the weighted three-mass automaton respectively, by Parts~V and~VI; Section~\ref{smc:tm:sec:frontquestions}.)
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Parts V and VI: conserved-mass thresholds (batch 80)}\label{smc:tm:sec:batch80}

\wtag\ This subsection and the four that follow were written on 2 October 2026, when four manuscripts of batch~80 of ProveIt's incoming reports were added to the report as Parts~\ref{smc:tm:part} and~\ref{smc:su:part}. They are called \emph{sources~14--17} after the file prefixes of their shipped programs and data, which continue this report's local sequence after~13. Their batch-80 manuscript numbers are different: \textbf{source~14 is batch-80 manuscript~10, source~15 is manuscript~02, source~16 is manuscript~05 and source~17 is manuscript~03}. A fifth archive of the batch, manuscript~06, is the superseded original of manuscript~05 and is not printed. Appendix~\ref{smc:app:provenance} gives the full provenance. None of the four manuscripts names a repository commit; source~16 cites Part~IV (source~13) by its title, for its post-elimination compiler, and sources~15 and~17 cite sources~14 and~16 as their ``companion'' reports. All four are dated 3 October 2026.

\toprule
Source & Batch~80 & Archive and title & Printed as\\
\midrule

15 & manuscript~02 & \path{Exact_Targets_Three_Mass_Units.zip}; \emph{Exact targets with three conserved mass units}, a declared extension of source~14 & Part~\ref{smc:tm:part}, Sections~\ref{smc:ct:sec:result}--\ref{smc:ct:sec:questions}\\

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Part~\ref{smc:tm:part} (sources~14 and~15), \emph{Three conserved mass units suffice}.} The model is a power-set alphabet $2^{\mathcal T}$ of Boolean channel types, the weight of a symbol being its cardinality, so that many distinct symbols have weight one. Source~14 compiles every separated reversible two-counter machine into a globally reversible, mass-conserving cellular automaton of forward and inverse radius at most four whose canonical configurations have total mass exactly three and gap $12\cdot2^a3^b$, and in which a clean one-cell symbol $\Ready(q_f)$ occurs exactly when the source halts (Theorem~\ref{smc:tm:thm:compiler}); radius one follows by four-site spatial blocking at the same clock, or by a four-phase dilation at four times the time. With Part~IV's mass-two theorem this makes three the least mass with fixed-rule undecidable pattern occurrence in that model (Theorem~\ref{smc:tm:thm:threshold}). Source~14 also gives exact type and collision-row counts, exact microtimes, and a quadratic natural-number certificate at a fixed source horizon with a unique witness (Theorem~\ref{smc:tm:thm:certificate}). Source~15 wraps a fresh-entry source so that it runs forward, then its logical inverse, and then relabels its state: for one fixed reversible cellular automaton, exact reachability $\exists t\,A^t(x_N)=y_N$ between two computable configurations of mass three and the same support is undecidable (Theorems~\ref{smc:ct:thm:main} and~\ref{smc:ct:thm:equiv}); it adds an exact first-target clock and a compact certificate whose natural zero fibres are in affine bijection with those of the full wrapped certificate (Theorem~\ref{smc:ct:thm:lift}).
\item \textbf{Part~\ref{smc:su:part} (sources~16 and~17), \emph{A single unit symbol}.} With at most one weight-one symbol, as in an ordinary integer-state number-conserving automaton, source~16 proves that the timed evolution relation at total mass at most three is effectively Presburger, uniformly in all positions and the time for each fixed rule (Theorem~\ref{smc:su:thm:main}); exact reachability and pattern occurrence are decidable, and horizon-free canonical quartics follow. Source~17 proves that exact reachability and pattern occurrence remain decidable at mass four (Theorem~\ref{smc:fm:thm:main}), but that the timed relation is no longer Presburger there: a binary radius-six shuttle hits an anchored pattern exactly at the times $k^2+(2d-11)k$ (Proposition~\ref{smc:fm:prop:hits}), with a one-witness quartic for that timing. For integer-state number-conserving automata the numerical particle lower bound is therefore five (Corollaries~\ref{smc:su:cor:numerical} and~\ref{smc:fm:cor:numerical}). (\wtag\ Added 3 October 2026: batch~82 adds a third source to Part~VI and a Part~VII, which extends the decidability at mass four to every dimension; Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:batch82}.)
\end{itemize}

The thresholds proved in this report, by model (``undecidable'' means fixed-rule undecidable for a computable family of initial configurations):

\small

\toprule

\midrule

At most one weight-one symbol (includes integer-state NCCA) & decidable (Part~IV) & decidable, timed relation Presburger (Part~VI, source~16) & decidable, timed relation not Presburger (Part~VI, source~17) & universal at large radius by the Alhazov--Imai abstract~\cite{alhazovimai}; reversibility not asserted, full paper not checked\\
\wtag\ The same, on $\Z^d$ for every finite $d$ (added 3 October 2026) & decidable (Part~VII, source~19) & decidable, fixed-input timed Presburger (source~19, Lemma~\ref{smc:zd:lem:triple}) & decidable (Theorem~\ref{smc:zd:thm:main}); timed relation not Presburger (source~17's shuttle embeds linewise) & source~19 infers universality by embedding the one-dimensional binary five-particle construction of batch-82 Reports~16 and~26 linewise (Corollary~\ref{smc:zd:cor:boundary}); Report~16 is Part~II of \path{five-particle-binary-automata}, Report~26 is placed there but not yet written, and this report does not establish it\\
\bottomrule

\end{center}

\textbf{How they relate to Parts~I--IV.} Both new Parts work in Part~IV's weighted model (Section~\ref{smc:sl:sec:diameter}: finite alphabet, finite radius, positive integer weights, a unique zero-weight vacuum, conservation on finite configurations), which allows several symbols of weight one. Part~IV's Theorem~\ref{smc:sl:thm:two} is the lower half of Part~V's threshold, and source~14 proves it again (a pointer replaces that proof, Section~\ref{smc:tm:sec:lower}). Part~VI restricts the same model to at most one weight-one symbol, so its mass-two statements are special cases of Part~IV's; source~16 reruns Part~IV's semilinearity architecture one mass higher, with a new no-split lemma, and reuses Part~IV's post-elimination compiler by name. The two new Parts do not contradict each other: Part~V keeps its finite control in weight-one controller types and changes its gap multiplicatively, neither of which a single stateless unit symbol permits, as sources~14 and~16 both say. Parts~V and~VI share no lemma with Parts~I--III, except that the certificate gate of sources~14 and~15 is the selector device of Part~III and of \emph{quadratic-orthant-certificates} (Section~\ref{smc:tm:sec:relation}).

\textbf{Morita and Imai again.} Part~IV and Part~V both rest on Morita and Imai's 2001 paper~\cite{moritaimai}, in different ways: Part~IV interfaces with their number-conserving reversible partitioned automaton itself (and corrects a sign in its rule (8.2)), while Part~V uses only their reversible two-counter normal form (Definitions~3.1--3.3, Proposition~3.4 and, for source~15, Lemma~3.5). No table, program or data file is shared, except that source~14 ships a byte copy of one source-13 receipt, which is not shipped twice.

Several words need the same care.
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Mass, unit, particle.} In Part~V a symbol is a set of Boolean channels and its mass is the number of channels, so a canonical configuration has \emph{three mass units in two occupied cells}. In an ordinary integer-state number-conserving automaton (NCCA) the state is its own mass, and a ``particle'' of the literature is one unit of numerical mass. The tempting false reading of Part~V as ``three particles suffice for an NCCA'' is refuted by Part~VI: with a single weight-one symbol, three and four units are decidable.
\item \textbf{Source and companion.} ``Source~14'' to ``source~17'' name manuscripts; inside Part~V ``the source'' is the simulated reversible two-counter machine. Source~15's ``companion construction'' is source~14, source~16's ``companion report'' is Part~IV (source~13), and source~17's ``companion'' is source~16; bracketed notes say so where they occur.
\item \textbf{Halt and target.} Source~14 observes the clean symbol $\Ready(q_f)$ \emph{somewhere}; source~15 asks for equality of the \emph{whole} configuration with an input-dependent target $y_N$; Part~VI's observations are exact targets and finite patterns, anchored or anywhere. Part~IV's pulse is an anchored first-pulse observation. None is global halting.
\item \textbf{Timed and untimed.} Source~16's relation includes the time and is uniform in all positions; source~17's mass-four description forgets the time and is for a fixed input, and the binary shuttle shows that the timed relation cannot be Presburger.
\item \textbf{Horizon.} Sources~14 and~15 fix an external source horizon $h$ (source steps, not CA ticks); sources~16 and~17 need no horizon. None of them is a fixed-arity polynomial for unbounded halting.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Relation of Parts V and VI to the repository}\label{smc:tm:sec:relation}

\wtag\ The four sources cite none of the material below except Part~IV, which source~16 cites by title; these cross-references are added. Results of other reports are cited by label name.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Re-derived results, printed as pointers.} Source~14's Section~\ref{smc:tm:sec:lower} proves Part~IV's Theorem~\ref{smc:sl:thm:two} again by the same argument; its proof is replaced by a pointer, and so are its bounded-diameter proposition (Proposition~\ref{smc:sl:prop:bounded}) and its finite-ring paragraph (Part~IV's Section~\ref{smc:sl:sec:diameter}); its two-mass test program is source~13's audit minus one line, and its receipt is byte-identical to source~13's, which is not shipped twice. Source~15's summary of the mass-two theorem is a pointer too, and its compiler interface restates source~14's results in its own words (Section~\ref{smc:ct:sec:interface}). Source~16's Lemma~\ref{smc:su:lem:walker} and Sections~\ref{smc:su:sec:core}--\ref{smc:su:sec:observation} follow the architecture of Part~IV's Sections~\ref{smc:sl:sec:two}--\ref{smc:sl:sec:semilinearity}, one mass higher, and its quartic is Part~IV's compiler (Section~\ref{smc:sl:sec:presburger-quartic}); source~17's Section~\ref{smc:fm:sec:packets} restates source~16. Each place carries a bracketed note; no novelty is claimed for any of them. New are source~16's no-split lemma (Lemma~\ref{smc:su:lem:invariant}), its mass-three core and uniform first-entry composition, and all of source~17 after its Section~\ref{smc:fm:sec:packets}.
\item \textbf{The certificate gate.} The product $(E_t-e_{tj})(e_{tj}+u_{tj})$ of sources~14 and~15 is the inactive-product gate of Lemma \texttt{qoc:rn:lem:gates} in Part~VI of \emph{quadratic-orthant-certificates}, and a form of Part~III's selector/copy device (note after Theorem~\ref{smc:cs:thm:packet}); source~14's natural-domain counterexample is consistent with that report's real-orthant one-hot lemma: one-hot selection survives over the nonnegative reals, divisibility does not.
\item \textbf{\emph{canonical-diophantine-certificates}, Part~XI}~\cite{cdc}. For source~16's semilinear relation, that Part's Theorem \texttt{cdc:of:thm:singlefoldsl} gives an orthant-nonnegative single-fold representation of degree two, as it does for Part~IV's (note after Corollary~\ref{smc:su:cor:quartic}). Its Theorem \texttt{cdc:of:thm:classification} ($\mathrm D^+_2=\mathrm D^+_3=\mathrm{SL}$) shows that source~17's non-semilinear hit set has no orthant-nonnegative representation of degree at most three, so the degree four of source~17's one-witness quartic is the least possible in that class (note after~\eqref{smc:fm:eq:quartic}).
\item \textbf{The programme's reviews.} The Hilbert-tenth-problem programme reviewed all five archives of this cluster before this write. Its review of sources~14 and~15~\cite{reviewthreemass} reports ``PASS within the stated model and interfaces'', with no repair, after both complete author replays and independent checks (360 literal core cases, 2,400 natural assignments, 45 compact/full zero-fibre correspondences, 7,968 microscopic cleanup ticks); it checked Morita and Imai's Proposition~3.4 and Lemma~3.5 in the primary paper (pp.~245--246) and records the off-zero and rational limitations of the compact certificate as documented limitations, not defects. Its review of sources~16 and~17 and of manuscript~06~\cite{reviewlowmass} reports ``PASS within the stated mathematical and executable scopes'', with no correction; it confirms that manuscript~05 changes only the attribution of manuscript~06 (the mathematical body is byte-identical), reconstructs all 8,192 local windows of the binary shuttle, checked Kong's thesis, could not certify the Japanese text of the KAKEN report or obtain the full Alhazov--Imai paper, and notes the five-witness refinement of source~16's quartic. Its index~\cite{reviewindexeighty} lists the reviews, and its placement audit~\cite{reviewmassplace} authenticates the 116 files placed by \texttt{345a9e44e}. Those reviews concern the archives and the placement; this write, which assembles the text, has not been reviewed by the programme. (\wtag\ Added 3 October 2026: the programme has since reviewed this write too, in \path{review_signal_batch80_typesetting_ef114b0bb.md} with the independent challenge \path{review_signal_batch80_quantifiers_ef114b0bb.md}, both in \path{Computability/HilbertTenthProblem/Papers/research-wip/native-stream-queue/} (commit \texttt{f787251a2}). They account for every formal statement, proof and display of sources~14--17 (71 statements and proofs retained and 4 printed as pointers; 98 displays retained and 7 declared deduplications) and confirm the degree-four minimality note after Proposition~\ref{smc:fm:prop:hits} in its stated class. Cooper's quantifier elimination, which source~16 imports, is the Lean development \path{Logic/PresburgerArithmetic} (Section~\ref{smc:cs:sec:relation}); that formalizes the imported step only.

Sources~16 and~17 credit Kong's 2021 thesis~\cite{kongthesis} (binary three-particle nonuniversality, with the four-particle case open in 2021), Imai's 2021 KAKEN report~\cite{kakenfinal} (decidability at most three particles, the problem left implicit), the Alhazov--Imai abstract~\cite{alhazovimai} (five particles, not asserted reversible) and a 2021 RIMS session~\cite{kongprogram,kaken}. Source~14 does not cite Kong or Imai's report, which concern the numerical model of Part~VI. No source claims priority, and this report claims none.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Status and what Parts V and VI do not claim}\label{smc:tm:sec:status}

\wtag\ The four sources give conventional proofs and finite exact-arithmetic checks, which are regression evidence, not proofs; all four are AI-assisted and unrefereed. Every limitation they state is kept in its Part. Collected here, they do not claim
\begin{itemize}
\item for source~14: an expanded universal source table, a numerical universal alphabet or row count, or a frozen universal transition table (universality is imported from Morita and Imai's Proposition~3.4); anchored halt observation or intrinsic universality; an efficiency bound (the gap is exponential in the counters); a three-numerical-particle theorem for integer-state NCCA; real or rational exactness of the certificate; a fixed-arity polynomial for an unknown horizon, or a finite-fold or single-fold representation; an exponentiation gadget (the loader is a bounded table); that the syntactic slot counts are lower bounds; novelty of the radius reductions; optimal overhead, a smallest alphabet, priority or literature-wide novelty; global halting, freezing or an eventual fixed point;
\item for source~15: one target independent of the input; that the target is stationary or reached once; microscopic reversal of the forward paths; a bound on the overhead of the fresh-entry normalization; a map of the whole natural orthant, an identity off the zero sets, or rational or real exactness of the lift; novelty or priority for forward-and-reverse computation; a numerical universal alphabet;
\item for source~16: one formula with the rule as a parameter; anything about intrinsic universality, signed masses, infinite alphabets, nonuniform or time-dependent rules, or active zero-weight backgrounds; a universal bound on the formula-dependent arity $K$, efficient preprocessing or optimal arity; a general rule-to-formula or quantifier-elimination implementation (the code validates the orbit accelerator); real or rational fibre exactness; a finite-fold conclusion for c.e.\ sets; novelty with respect to Kong's thesis and Imai's report;
\item for source~17: an efficiency bound; infinite backgrounds, signed weights, infinite alphabets, active zero-weight backgrounds, several unit labels, or time-dependent or nonuniform rules; a five-particle upper bound for binary automata; an implemented rule-to-section compiler or a formalization; reversibility of the binary shuttle (it is not injective); a general fixed-arity single-fold quartic compiler; uniform timed Presburger definability at mass four; priority, or any statement on the present status of the four-particle question;
\item a proof-assistant verification; none of the sources' code is a verified compiler.
\end{itemize}
The report claims no novelty for the results that the four sources prove again (Section~\ref{smc:tm:sec:relation}).

\subsection{Research questions answered or touched by Parts V and VI}\label{smc:tm:sec:frontquestions}

\wtag\ Dated notes at the questions themselves record the following; Table~\ref{smc:tab:questions} carries the same information in its last rows.
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Part~IV's remark after Theorem~\ref{smc:sl:thm:two}} (``It does not show that three suffices, or identify a least universal mass'') is \emph{answered, model by model}. With several weight-one symbols three suffices, for pattern occurrence (Theorem~\ref{smc:tm:thm:threshold}) and for exact pairs (Theorem~\ref{smc:ct:thm:main}); with a single weight-one symbol neither three nor four does (Theorems~\ref{smc:su:thm:main} and~\ref{smc:fm:thm:main}), so that the least universal numerical mass is at least five, and exactly five if the Alhazov--Imai construction holds as its abstract states (its full paper has not been checked, and it is not asserted reversible). (\wtag\ Added 3 October 2026, batch~82: the lower bound five holds in every finite dimension (Part~\ref{smc:zd:part}, Theorem~\ref{smc:zd:thm:main}); source~19 states an inherited sharp four-versus-five boundary in every dimension, conditional on batch-82 Reports~16 and~26, which this report does not print: Report~16 is Part~II of \path{five-particle-binary-automata}, whose Part~I (Reports~14 and~15) proves the binary one-dimensional upper bound five, and Report~26 is placed there but not yet written.)
\item \textbf{Part~IV's fourth question} (``Resolve small-particle model comparisons'') is \emph{answered in substance}: the number of weight-one symbols is the decisive convention (Section~\ref{smc:tm:sec:notation}). Still open: the full Alhazov--Imai construction, a reversible numerical threshold, a binary upper bound, and the least number of weight-one symbols that permits a universal three-mass construction.
\item \textbf{Part~IV's fifth question} (``Keep exact targets separate from halt signals'') is \emph{answered for the three-mass weighted automaton} by source~15, whose output normalization is inverse execution from a fresh entry state; it is not answered for Part~IV's own Morita--Imai pulse interface.
\item \textbf{Part~IV's non-claims} ``exact-target undecidability, \ldots a minimum universal mass, or that three particles suffice'' (Section~\ref{smc:cs:sec:status}) remain true of Part~IV; Parts~V and~VI supply those statements in the models stated above.
\item \textbf{Part~IV's first question} (``Print one safe universal source'') is \emph{not} answered: sources~14 and~15 also leave the universal source as an existence construction (their first questions).
\item \textbf{Source~14's third question} (exact finite targets need explicit treatment of the final counters) is answered by source~15; its anchored halt observer stays open. \textbf{Source~14's and source~15's fourth questions} (the integer-state mass convention) are answered in part by Part~VI (at least five units for undecidable observations); the sharp reversible threshold stays open. \textbf{Source~16's ``four-versus-five boundary''} is answered by source~17 within its class.
\item All other questions of Parts~V and~VI (Sections~\ref{smc:tm:sec:questions}, \ref{smc:ct:sec:questions} and~\ref{smc:fm:sec:checks}, and source~16's two directions in Section~\ref{smc:su:sec:sources}) are open. (\wtag\ Added 3 October 2026: source~17's seventh question is now answered for a fixed input by source~18, and its second, fourth and sixth are touched by sources~18, 20 and~21; Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:frontquestions}.)
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Source 18 and Part VII: mass at most four in every dimension (batch 82)}\label{smc:zd:sec:batch82}

\wtag\ This subsection and the four that follow were written on 3 October 2026, when four manuscripts of batch~82 of ProveIt's incoming reports were added to the report: one as a third source of Part~\ref{smc:su:part}, the other three as Part~\ref{smc:zd:part}. They are called \emph{sources~18--21} after the file prefixes of their shipped programs and data, which continue this report's local sequence after~17. Their batch-82 manuscript numbers are different, and so are the report numbers of the research pipeline that wrote them: \textbf{source~18 is batch-82 manuscript~19 (the pipeline's addendum~13), source~19 is manuscript~06 (Report~29), source~20 is manuscript~17 (Report~30) and source~21 is manuscript~01 (Report~31)}; source~17 is the pipeline's Report~12. All four arrived in commit \texttt{db37d18c8} and are dated 3 October 2026; Appendix~\ref{smc:app:provenance} gives the full provenance. None names a repository commit.

Citations among them, all pinned by SHA-256 and verified at placement: source~18 cites source~17; source~19 cites sources~17 and~18 and batch-82 Reports~16 and~26; source~20 cites source~19; source~21 cites sources~17, 18 and~20. Reports~16 and~26 belong to the binary five-particle line of the same batch and were placed in the collection's report \path{five-particle-binary-automata} (commits \texttt{bca6383e9} and \texttt{7d2b1b245}); Report~16 is printed there as Part~II, and Report~26 is not yet written. Neither is printed here.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Source~18 (Part~\ref{smc:su:part}), \emph{timed quartics for a fixed input}.} Conditional on source~17's effective normal form (Proposition~\ref{smc:fm:prop:normal}), the complete timed orbit of a fixed input of mass at most four is a disjoint union of half-open charts with at most two natural parameters, affine domains and quadratic outputs (Lemma~\ref{smc:tq:lem:interface}), and a canonical quartic with $B+K+M$ natural witnesses has exactly one witness tuple at each complete timed configuration (Theorem~\ref{smc:tq:thm:main}, Proposition~\ref{smc:tq:prop:compiler}). For source~17's binary shuttle it writes the two charts explicitly and specializes the certificate to four witnesses and seven squares, exact even for nonnegative real witnesses and uniform in the gap (Corollary~\ref{smc:tq:cor:uniform}). It answers source~17's last question for a fixed input (Question~\ref{smc:fm:q:quartic}).
\item \textbf{Source~19 (Part~\ref{smc:zd:part}), \emph{every dimension}.} For a conservative cellular automaton on $\Z^d$ with a finite positively weighted alphabet, a unique vacuum and at most one weight-one symbol, exact and translated reachability and anchored and translated pattern occurrence are decidable from every input of mass at most four, uniformly in~$d$ and without reversibility (Theorem~\ref{smc:zd:thm:main}); its appendix lifts source~18's timed quartics to $\Z^d$ (Theorem~\ref{smc:zd:thm:quartic}). Its sharp four-versus-five boundary (Corollary~\ref{smc:zd:cor:boundary}) is conditional on Reports~16 and~26.
\item \textbf{Source~20, \emph{orbit geometry and counting}.} For a fixed input of mass at most four the stationary-frame visited set is semilinear, lies in finitely many translates of one rational plane of rank at most two, and has eventually quasipolynomial box counts of sharp degree $\rank_\Q\{E,\nu\}$ (Theorem~\ref{smc:og:thm:geometry}); an expanding shuttle discovers sites at the rate $T^{r/2}$ and has piecewise quadratic first arrivals (Theorem~\ref{smc:og:thm:timing}); a nonzero drift can destroy semilinearity and quasipolynomiality (Theorem~\ref{smc:og:thm:drift}), as an explicit weighted radius-six planar automaton shows with the exact count $3N-3\sqrt N+O(1)$ (Theorem~\ref{smc:og:thm:exactdrift}); canonical first-visit quartics follow (Theorem~\ref{smc:og:thm:quartic}).
\item \textbf{Source~21, \emph{a binary planar shuttle}.} An ordinary binary, quiescent, conservative radius-six automaton on $\Z^2$, obtained by lifting two outputs of source~17's binary shuttle by one row, has a four-particle orbit with source~17's clock (Theorem~\ref{smc:bp:thm:orbit}), visited set a wedge minus two lines (Theorem~\ref{smc:bp:thm:visited}), exact stationary and drifted counts $4N-4\sqrt N+O_k(1)$ (Theorems~\ref{smc:bp:thm:Gcount} and~\ref{smc:bp:thm:Fcount}), Boolean degree exactly~45 with exactly 78 essential cells (Theorem~\ref{smc:bp:thm:degree}), and a one-step quartic with 614 unique auxiliaries (Theorem~\ref{smc:bp:thm:quartic}).
\end{itemize}

The threshold table of Section~\ref{smc:tm:sec:batch80} gains a row for every finite dimension (\wtag, 3 October 2026). Its entry at mass five records only what is true now: the inherited five-particle upper bound in every dimension rests on source~19's linewise embedding and on Reports~16 and~26; Report~16 is Part~II of \path{five-particle-binary-automata}, Report~26 is placed there but not yet written, and the programme's reviews of both leave published theorems as explicit dependencies (note after Corollary~\ref{smc:zd:cor:boundary}).

Several readings need the same care.
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{The gap and the dimension.} In sources~17 and~18 the letter $d$ is the initial right-marker coordinate of the binary shuttle; in sources~19 and~20 it is the dimension. Source~21 calls the initial gap $k\ge7$; its $k$ is source~17's and source~18's $d$, and its $d=k+n$ is source~18's $r=d+n$. The tempting reading of source~18's ``$d\ge7$'' as a dimension bound is false.
\item \textbf{Particles and mass.} In source~21 every occupied site has weight one, so ``four particles'' is mass four. Source~20's explicit automata have mass four in three occupied sites: two unit markers and one head of weight two. Its letters $E,W$ are head states; source~21's $E,W,R,L$ are names of rules of a binary automaton.
\item \textbf{Timed and untimed, fixed and uniform.} Source~18's relation keeps the time and the complete configuration; source~20's first-visit certificates forget the time and keep one witness per site. Every certificate of the four sources is for one fixed rule and one fixed input, with an arity that depends on the input; source~16's uniform Presburger description (Theorem~\ref{smc:su:thm:main}) has no analogue here at mass four.
\item \textbf{One step and one orbit.} Source~21's 614-auxiliary quartic certifies one local transition of its rule; source~18's and source~20's quartics certify one orbit.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Relation of source 18 and Part VII to the repository}\label{smc:zd:sec:relation}

\wtag\ The four sources cite only Part~VI's source~17, each other and the batch-82 Reports~16 and~26; the cross-references below are added. Results of other reports are cited by label name.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Re-derived results, printed as pointers or second routes.} Source~18 restates source~17's binary rule and its conservation certificate (a pointer replaces that subsection, Section~\ref{smc:tq:sec:binary}), its return quartic~\eqref{smc:fm:eq:quartic} and its normal form (notes in Sections~\ref{smc:tq:sec:interface} and~\ref{smc:tq:sec:uniform}), and ships three of source~17's files byte for byte (shipped once). Its compiler is a second route to Part~I's Theorem~\ref{smc:cg:thm:quarticunion}, with Part~V's inactive gate and Lemma \texttt{qoc:rn:lem:gates} of \emph{quadratic-orthant-certificates} (note in Section~\ref{smc:tq:sec:compiler}). Source~19 proves source~17's Theorem~\ref{smc:fm:thm:main} again at $d=1$ by the same architecture while extending it to $\Z^d$ (notes from Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:statement} on); its Lemma~\ref{smc:zd:lem:triple} is a fixed-input, $\Z^d$ form of source~16's Theorem~\ref{smc:su:thm:main}; its mass-two case meets Part~IV's Theorem~\ref{smc:sl:thm:two}; its appendix restates source~18's chart partition and compiler (printed in full, with notes). Source~20's canonical membership quartic is a second route to Part~IV's compiler (Section~\ref{smc:sl:sec:presburger-quartic}) and to Theorem \texttt{cdc:of:thm:singlefoldsl} of \emph{canonical-diophantine-certificates} (note in Appendix~\ref{smc:og:app:quartic}); its Lemma~\ref{smc:og:lem:affine} is classical. Source~21's orbit theorem is the planar lift of source~17's Proposition~\ref{smc:fm:prop:hits}, and its one-step quartic uses Part~IV's device of gate residuals. Each place carries a bracketed note, and no novelty is claimed for any of them.
\item \textbf{New in the four sources.} Source~18's complete-orbit chart partition, constant-term lifting, ledgers, selector elimination, signed external pairs and the four-witness binary certificate; source~19's actual-site contact, exact contact rays, finite nonparallel switch set, compact triples that miss, the $\Z^d$ dispatch and drift cutoff, and the $\Z^d$ chart refinements; all of source~20's geometry, counting, explicit automata and first-visit certificates beyond the membership compiler; source~21's binary planar rule with its full-shift conservation proof, wedge, counts, exact dependency set and one-step quartic.
\item \textbf{Degree four is least for source~18's binary certificates.} The note after~\eqref{smc:fm:eq:quartic} transfers. The complete timed relation of the binary shuttle meets the semilinear condition $(x_0,x_1,x_2)=(0,3,4)$ exactly at the return times $T(n)$ of Section~\ref{smc:tq:sec:binary}, which form a set that is not semilinear (Proposition~\ref{smc:fm:prop:hits}); so the relation is not semilinear, and by Theorem \texttt{cdc:of:thm:classification} it has no natural-witness representation of degree at most three by a polynomial nonnegative on the real nonnegative orthant. This observation is \wtag, made at this merge; the programme has not reviewed it.
\item \textbf{The programme's reviews.} The Hilbert's-tenth programme reviewed three of the four sources on the day of their placement, in \path{Computability/HilbertTenthProblem/Papers/research-wip/native-stream-queue/}. Source~21: \path{review_binary_planar_four_particle31.md} (commit \texttt{16f50dcc6})~\cite{reviewplanar}, ``PASS within the stated scope; no error found'', with an independent reconstruction of the whole local quartic from the four printed rules (Section~\ref{smc:bp:sec:verification}). Source~19: \path{review_dimension_independent_four_mass.md} (commit \texttt{7b547f686})~\cite{reviewzd}: the proof of Theorem~\ref{smc:zd:thm:main} passes, a second reviewer found no gap, and the inherited five-particle upper bound is not reproved (Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:repro}). Source~20: a scoped intake, \path{review_original_frame_first_hit30_intake.md} (commit \texttt{c875bad40})~\cite{reviewfirsthit}, which finds no defect in the whole-orbit-injectivity step of Corollary~\ref{smc:og:cor:original-target} but is not a review of the compiler, and names source~19's timed companion proof as the next audit; a follow-up (\path{original_frame_first_hit30_fixture.md} with its independent review \path{review_original_frame_first_hit30_fixture.md}, commit \texttt{5b4c091e6})~\cite{reviewfixture} then read that proof in full, found it sound as a conditional statement, and emitted a complete 35-operation first-hit quartic for one drifted orbit of source~20's weighted planar automaton (note in Appendix~\ref{smc:og:app:quartic}). Source~18 has not been reviewed: the programme's archive inventory \path{reviewed_report_archive_replay.md} lists its archive as relocation only. The programme has also reviewed Reports~16 and~26, on which source~19's corollary rests (commits \texttt{69730d3e8}~\cite{reviewsource16} and \texttt{fb7e3cb47}~\cite{reviewparallel}; note after Corollary~\ref{smc:zd:cor:boundary}). The batch-80 review of source~17~\cite{reviewlowmass}, whose normal form sources~18--20 import, still applies to that import. This write has not been reviewed by the programme.
\item \textbf{The repository's universal bounds.} The project README's fixed-universal operation bounds~\cite{repo} are untouched: all quartics of the four sources are for one fixed rule and one fixed input, with arity depending on the input, or for one local transition (source~21); the programme's review of source~21 says the same for it.
\item \textbf{Formal status.} Nothing in source~18 or Part~VII is formalized in Lean or Rocq, and the Hilbert-tenth-problem Lean development has no cellular-automaton module. The Presburger decision step that source~19 applies to its explicit formulas is Cooper's quantifier elimination, formalized in \path{Logic/PresburgerArithmetic} (Section~\ref{smc:cs:sec:relation}); that formalizes the imported step only. Woods' counting theorems, which source~20 uses, are not formalized in the repository.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Status and what source 18 and Part VII do not claim}\label{smc:zd:sec:status}

\wtag\ The four sources give conventional proofs and finite exact-arithmetic checks, which are regression evidence, not proofs; all four are AI-assisted and unrefereed. Every limitation they state is kept in its place. Collected here, they do not claim
\begin{itemize}
\item for source~18: a proof of the imported normal form (the theorem is conditional on it); an implemented rule-to-chart preprocessing, a decision of the semantic bijectivity of a chart family, or a complexity bound; a bound uniform over inputs, uniform timed Presburger definability, or a single-fold or finite-fold Diophantine conclusion, including for MRDP; real or rational exactness of the generic compiler (only the four-witness binary certificate is exact for nonnegative real witnesses, and unrestricted real witnesses give spurious zeros); uniqueness after forgetting the time; witness minimality; novelty, priority or a literature status;
\item for source~19: uniform timed Presburger definability; a useful complexity bound or small libraries; a general rule compiler or an end-to-end simulation of every input; reversibility; the inherited five-particle upper bound, which it does not reconstruct (Corollary~\ref{smc:zd:cor:boundary} is conditional); several unit labels, negative or zero weights, infinite alphabets, an active vacuum, infinite scenery or time- or position-dependent rules; untimed finite-foldness, a single-fold MRDP theorem, a uniform arity bound or real or rational exactness for its appendix; a formalization; novelty or priority;
\item for source~20: a binary or reversible realization; new theorems about Presburger arithmetic (its counting steps apply Woods' theorems); practical algorithms or size bounds; an exhaustive asymptotic taxonomy in the original frame; a general original-frame first-hit certificate for sites or patterns under drift; a single-fold MRDP theorem, a uniform arity bound or real or rational exactness; an efficient general compiler, a formalization or a universality threshold; novelty with respect to the pebble-automaton literature;
\item for source~21: reversibility or universality of either rule; a smallest mass, radius, number of essential cells, Boolean degree or auxiliary count; a classification of malformed orbits; an unbounded-time or finite-fold representation from its local quartic; a real-witness conclusion inherited from source~20;
\item a proof-assistant verification; none of the sources' code is a verified compiler.
\end{itemize}
The report claims no novelty for what the four sources prove again (Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:relation}).

\subsection{Research questions answered or touched by source 18 and Part VII}\label{smc:zd:sec:frontquestions}

\wtag\ Dated notes at the questions themselves record the following; Table~\ref{smc:tab:questions} carries the same information in its last rows.
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Source~17's seventh question} (Question~\ref{smc:fm:q:quartic}, a fixed-input timed quartic compiler from disjoint phases) is \emph{answered for a fixed input} by source~18, conditional on Proposition~\ref{smc:fm:prop:normal} and with natural witnesses; source~19's Appendix~\ref{smc:zd:app:quartic} lifts the answer to $\Z^d$. The rule-to-chart preprocessing is not implemented.
\item \textbf{Source~17's second and fourth questions} (uniform untimed spatial definability; a stronger normal form for original-frame timed relations) are \emph{touched}: for a fixed input the visited set is semilinear with quasipolynomial counts (source~20), the complete timed orbit is a disjoint union of quadratic charts (source~18), and with drift original-frame site sets can be nonsemilinear (Theorem~\ref{smc:og:thm:exactdrift}). The uniform versions stay open.
\item \textbf{Source~17's sixth question} (minimal radius for the binary timing example) is \emph{touched} by source~21: its planar analogue has exact radius six and exactly 78 essential cells, with no minimality claimed; the one-dimensional question stays open.
\item \textbf{Source~17's first, third and fifth questions} stay open; source~19 adds literature (Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:literature}), shows by an example that naive geometric bounds fail (Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:exception}), and leaves several unit labels open (its Question~\ref{smc:zd:q:units}).
