\phantomsection\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Source 19: Dimension independent particle thresholds}
\noindent{\large\bfseries Source 19 (batch-82 manuscript 06): Dimension independent particle thresholds}\par
\noindent\emph{Effective reachability at mass four and the inherited mass five boundary}, ``Research report 29'', 3 October 2026. \wtag\ From the archive \path{Dimension_Independent_Particle_Thresholds_Package.zip}; its labels carry the prefix \texttt{smc:zd:}, its sections are Sections~\ref{smc:zd:sec:statement}--\ref{smc:zd:sec:questions} here, and its two appendices are Appendices~\ref{smc:zd:app:quartic} and~\ref{smc:zd:app:lineage}, after Part~III's. The Hilbert's-tenth programme has reviewed its proof (note at the end of Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:repro}). Its abstract follows; the bracketed notes in it are \wtag.
\begin{quote}\small
For a translation invariant finite range cellular automaton on $\Z^d$, with a finite positively weighted alphabet, a unique weight zero vacuum, and at most one weight one symbol, exact finite configuration reachability and finite pattern occurrence are decidable from every input of mass at most four. The result is uniform in the finite dimension and does not require reversibility. A mass two packet has finitely many normalized shapes. Its successful flights toward a stationary unit follow finitely many exact lattice rays. Two consecutive successful flights in nonparallel directions force the marker separation into an effectively computable finite set. Outside that set, the dynamics reduces to finite control and one additive scalar gap, with the vector anchor retained as output. Expanding cycles have quadratic section times; a drift cutoff restores anchored observations in the original frame. We give the full effective proof, all weighted and no unit cases, a decision algorithm, and reproducible finite arithmetic checks. An appendix extends Report 13's [source~18's] fixed input timed quartic certificate compiler using canonical higher dimensional phase charts. The previously established one dimensional five particle universal construction embeds linewise into every finite dimension, giving the inherited sharp four versus five boundary [conditional on batch-82 Reports~16 and~26, which this report does not print; note after Corollary~\ref{smc:zd:cor:boundary}]. This is an ordinary mathematical proof, not a formalization, an implemented general rule compiler, or a claim of novelty or priority.
\end{quote}

\section{Statement and scope}\label{smc:zd:sec:statement}
The mass of a finite configuration $c\colon\Z^d\to A$ is
\[
 \mathsf m(c)=\sum_{x\in\Z^d}w(c(x)).
\]
Here $A$ is finite, $0\in A$ is the unique weight zero symbol, and every other symbol has a positive integer weight. A finite configuration equals $0$ outside a finite support. A cellular automaton (CA) is deterministic, translation equivariant, and has a finite neighborhood. Conservation below means conservation of this finite total mass for every finite configuration. Write $\tau_v c(x)=c(x-v)$.

\begin{theorem}[Uniform finite input decision theorem]\label{smc:zd:thm:main}
Let $d\ge1$ be finite. Let $F$ be a CA on $\Z^d$ with a quiescent vacuum and the weights just specified. Assume mass conservation and at most one weight one symbol. Given its finite local rule, a finite neighborhood bound, the weights, and a finite input $c$ of mass at most four, there is an effective procedure deciding each of the following:
\begin{enumerate}
\item whether $F^t(c)=b$ for a specified finite configuration $b$ and some $t\in\NN$;
\item whether $F^t(c)=\tau_v b$ for some $t\in\NN$ and $v\in\Z^d$;
\item whether a specified finite pattern occurs in its prescribed window at some time;
\item whether that pattern occurs at some translated window at some time.
\end{enumerate}
Pattern zeros are enforced. Reversibility is not required. The rule hypotheses are promises; the procedure need not certify conservation for an arbitrary submitted rule.
\end{theorem}

\wtag\ [For $d=1$ this is Theorem~\ref{smc:fm:thm:main} of Part~VI (source~17, the pipeline's ``Report~12''), whose proof the present one generalizes step by step: the stationary-unit frame, bounded packets, the tagged seed library with $B$ fixed first, the thresholds $N$ before $W$, one additive gap, the dispatch over the five mass partitions, termination in three forms, and the drift cutoff. Brackets below mark each place; at $d=1$ the steps there are a re-proof by the same architecture, and no novelty is claimed for them. The higher-dimensional ingredients are new: contact through actual occupied sites rather than a bounding box (Figure~\ref{smc:zd:fig:geometry}), exact contact rays with a Bezout functional and residues (Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:rays}), the finite nonparallel switch set (Lemma~\ref{smc:zd:lem:exception}), compact triples that miss a remote unit (Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:compact}), and a transverse offset folded into the finite mode (Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:counter}). Source~19 itself cites source~17 only in Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:boundary} and in its lineage appendix.]

The proof constructs a fixed input orbit description. In the frame fixing isolated mass one particles, its untimed part is effectively Presburger definable. Its eventual form is independent finite phase objects, a whole configuration periodic up to translation, or an expanding one counter shuttle. In the last case exact elapsed time is retained, but is quadratic in the shuttle cycle index. No assertion of uniform timed Presburger definability is made.

The input dimension may be part of the finite rule description. Every construction below is a finite enumeration or effective integer calculation for that dimension. No useful complexity bound is asserted. In particular, ``effective'' does not mean that the normalized state libraries or resulting formula are small.

\begin{corollary}[Inherited sharp boundary]\label{smc:zd:cor:boundary}
In the effective finite input and finite observation simulation setting of Reports 16 and 26 \textup{[batch-82 reports of the same pipeline; note below]}, the sharp conserved mass boundary is four versus five in every finite dimension: mass at most four has the decision property of Theorem~\ref{smc:zd:thm:main}, and the previously established binary five particle universal construction embeds in every $\Z^d$.
\end{corollary}
The upper part inherits the source construction and compiler proofs in those reports; this report proves the higher dimensional lower part and the linewise inheritance argument. Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:boundary} states the distinction precisely. It does not transfer undecidability to an arbitrary observation convention lacking the inherited effective encoding and halt observation.

\wtag\ [The upper half of Corollary~\ref{smc:zd:cor:boundary} is not proved in this report. It is the linewise embedding (Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:boundary}) of the binary five-particle universal construction of batch-82 Reports~16 and~26 of the same research pipeline, which source~19 pins by SHA-256 (Appendix~\ref{smc:zd:app:lineage}). Both were placed in the collection's report \path{five-particle-binary-automata} (file prefixes \path{16-literal-source-} and \path{26-parallel-involutions-}; placement commits \texttt{bca6383e9} and \texttt{7d2b1b245}). Report~16 is printed there as Part~II (labels \texttt{fpa:ls:}); Part~I of that report, Reports~14 and~15, proves with source~17 that five particles are the exact one-dimensional binary threshold (its Corollary \texttt{fpa:bc:cor:sharp}). Report~26 is placed there but not yet written. The four-versus-five boundary in every dimension is therefore sharp only conditionally on those releases having their stated properties. The Hilbert's-tenth programme has reviewed both. Its review of Report~16 (\path{review_literal_universal_reversible_source16.md}, commit \texttt{69730d3e8})~\cite{reviewsource16} found no source-interface or arithmetic contradiction; it records that Report~16 closes the literal-source and clean-nonblocking obligations of the earlier five-particle compiler, keeps the published Neary--Woods universality theorem and the earlier compiler theorems as explicit dependencies, and notes that Report~16's own threshold claim inherits source~17's mass-four theorem, which it does not recertify. Its review of Reports~26--28 (\path{review_parallel_particle_reports.md}, commit \texttt{fb7e3cb47})~\cite{reviewparallel} found the two-involution mechanism of Report~26 sound but states that it did not reconstruct the complete source compiler and that Report~26's full source binding remains outside its replay. The lower half, decidability at mass at most four in every finite dimension, is proved in full here. Source~19's own adversarial review says the same: ``the sharpness statement remains conditional on the cited five-particle source releases having their stated properties'' (\path{19-mass-four-zd-independent-review.md}).]

\section{Stationary unit frame and independent components}\label{smc:zd:sec:frame}
Use the maximum norm on $\Z^d$. A radius bound $R\ge0$ means that the output at $x$ depends only on $x+[-R,R]^d$. For a nonempty finite configuration,
\[
 \diam(c)=\max\{\inorm{x-y}:x,y\in\supp(c)\}.
\]
Normalize it by subtracting its lexicographically first occupied site. Translation preserves lexicographic order, so this normalization is translation compatible. Bounded diameter and bounded mass leave only finitely many normalized configurations, and their successors can be computed by evaluating the rule on a finite neighborhood of their support.

Suppose the unique possible weight one symbol $u$ exists. An isolated $u$ must evolve to exactly one $u$: positivity and conservation leave no other output of mass one. Quiescence and finite range confine its displacement $\delta$ to $\inorm\delta\le R$. Put
\[
 G=\tau_{-\delta}F,\qquad S=\max(1,R+\inorm\delta).
\]
Then $G$ has radius at most $S$, fixes each isolated $u$, and
\begin{equation}\label{smc:zd:eq:restore}
 F^t=\tau_{t\delta}G^t.
\end{equation}
The case with no unit symbol is treated separately in Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:nounit}.

Join occupied sites at distance at most $2S$. A connected component of mass $m$ has at most $m$ occupied sites and therefore diameter at most $2S(m-1)$. Components mutually farther than $2S$ have disjoint radius $S$ influence neighborhoods. Their one step evolutions are independent and have disjoint output supports. By induction, the independent evolutions agree with the full evolution through the first configuration at which two such supports have distance at most $2S$. The next step is computed using the full rule. A geometric contact need not imply a nontrivial collision, but processing it exactly is harmless.

This observation is used with \emph{actual occupied sites}. In several dimensions an expanded bounding box is generally larger than the union of the contact boxes of those sites. Replacing the latter by the former is incorrect.

\section{Bounded mass two packets}\label{smc:zd:sec:dimer}
\wtag\ [At $d=1$ the lemma below is source~17's Lemma~\ref{smc:fm:lem:two}, which restates source~16's no-split Lemma~\ref{smc:su:lem:invariant}; the coordinatewise intersection of two radius-$S$ cubes is the $\Z^d$ form of that argument. With one weight-one symbol and $d=1$, the mass-two case is also a special case of Part~IV's Theorem~\ref{smc:sl:thm:two}, proved there for any number of weight-one symbols.]
\begin{lemma}\label{smc:zd:lem:dimer}
A mass two configuration of diameter at most $2S$ remains within that diameter under $G$. It has an effectively computable finite transient and a periodic profile up to a vector translation.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A weight two singleton produces support in its radius $S$ cube, of diameter at most $2S$. Otherwise the configuration is two units, translated to $0$ and $v$, with $\inorm v\le2S$.

If $\inorm v>S$, each original occupied site sees only its own unit and stays occupied by $u$. These two sites already exhaust the total mass, so the configuration is fixed. If $\inorm v\le S$, any newly occupied site other than those two must see both units: a neighborhood seeing zero units gives vacuum, and a neighborhood seeing just one gives the stationary singleton output. The intersection of the two radius $S$ cubes contains both original sites, and its coordinate widths are $2S-|v_i|\le2S$. The entire successor support lies in that intersection.

There are finitely many normalized mass two shapes of this diameter. Each has one next normalized shape and a translation increment. A repeated shape gives the finite transient followed, after resetting the time origin, by
\begin{equation}\label{smc:zd:eq:packet}
 P(r+kp)=kD+P_r,\qquad 0\le r<p,\quad k\in\NN,
\end{equation}
where $D\in\Z^d$, the period $p\ge1$, and all labeled phase shapes $P_r$ are explicit. This finite graph can be enumerated.
\end{proof}

A pair of units at distance greater than $2S$ is fixed. The lemma includes all weight two singleton labels and two unit shapes, so it is not restricted to binary alphabets. The word ``packet'' will denote such a bounded mass two object, and ``dimer'' will be used synonymously even when its support is a singleton.

\section{Mass three profiles and a finite encounter library}\label{smc:zd:sec:triple}
\begin{lemma}\label{smc:zd:lem:triple}
Every fixed mass three input has an effective finite prefix followed by either a whole configuration periodic up to a vector translation, or an independent stationary unit and a finite phase mass two packet. Three stationary units are included as a constant case. Mass at most three consequently has a fixed input timed Presburger description.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Take as the core every normalized mass three configuration of diameter at most $4S$. Outside this finite core, the $2S$ component partition is $2+1$ or $1+1+1$. The latter is fixed. In the former case Lemma~\ref{smc:zd:lem:dimer} gives the dimer's finite transient and phases. In an eventual phase its occupied coordinates are $p+kD$ for a finite list of offsets $p$, while the other unit is stationary.

Entry of the complete configuration into the diameter $4S$ core is a finite conjunction of coordinatewise linear inequalities in $k$. Their common integer solution set is an interval, possibly empty or unbounded. Compute its first nonnegative integer, compare the corresponding actual times over all phases, and also check the finite transient. If no core entry exists, there is no later dimer unit contact: such a contact would be connected and have diameter at most $4S$.

From each normalized core state, take one exact step and then its just computed first return when needed. The result is a finite deterministic graph of core returns, labeled by finite elapsed times and translation vectors, with possible terminal independent tails. Repetition of a normalized core state gives equality of two complete configurations up to translation. Determinism and equivariance propagate this equality to all future times, including every intermediate excursion. The corresponding finite period can be enumerated even if enormous.

In the independent case, take a common period for the finite phase objects. In either case coordinates and time become affine in one nonnegative integer after finitely many phases. Restoring $\delta t$ keeps them affine.
\end{proof}
\wtag\ [In $d=1$, source~16 proves the stronger uniform form, Theorem~\ref{smc:su:thm:main} (uniform in all positions and the time), and source~17's Lemma~\ref{smc:fm:lem:three} is the fixed-input form used here. Lemma~\ref{smc:zd:lem:triple} is weaker in $d=1$ but holds in every $\Z^d$, and source~19 needs only the fixed-input form.]

\wtag\ [At $d=1$ the tagged seed library and its bound $B$ are source~17's (Section~\ref{smc:fm:sec:library}).] Enumerate every normalized \emph{connected} mass three seed. Its diameter is at most $4S$, so this is a finite effective list. A dimer's first contact with a unit is such a seed. Include a finite bookkeeping tag for the incoming target unit's coordinate. The tag records the geometric role of that coordinate; it introduces neither a new CA symbol nor a persistent physical identity.

For each tagged seed, compute the preceding lemma's profile and extend its finite prefix to one of two checkpoints:
\begin{description}[style=nextline,leftmargin=0pt,labelsep=0pt]
\item[Compact] The whole triple is periodic up to translation, with finitely many bounded phase representatives.
\item[Emission] A stationary unit remains and the independent dimer has nonzero drift $D$. From the checkpoint onward it is purely periodic up to $D$ and never comes within $2S$ of the residual unit.
\end{description}
If an independent dimer has $D=0$, the complete triple is eventually periodic and belongs to Compact. If $D\ne0$, choose a coordinate with nonzero drift. Its linear escape allows the finite prefix to be extended effectively until every later phase remains beyond the residual unit's contact interval. This produces Emission.

Use a common seed anchor frame: normalize each triple seed with occupied anchor $0$, and measure every occupied coordinate through its checkpoint in that frame. Choose $B$ to bound all these coordinates, every compact phase representative, and every emission first period representative, including residual unit offsets. On reanchoring an emission at its residual unit, include all resulting finite difference offsets as well, enlarging $B$ if necessary. The list is finite. This $B$ is computed solely from the original connected mass three seeds, \emph{before} any mass four core is chosen. Compact phase diameters are at most $2B$.

\section{Compact triples reset or escape}\label{smc:zd:sec:compact}
Place a tagged library seed at an anchor $a$ and a fourth unit at $b$, with $\inorm{b-a}>B+2S$. Its entire precomputed prefix through the checkpoint is independent of the fourth unit. At an emission, the remote unit remains fixed; the new residual unit is at a seed dependent fixed offset from the target anchor.

For a compact checkpoint, compute the first contact with the fourth unit by enumerating the compact phases and the actual occupied sites in each. For each candidate site the distance at most $2S$ conditions are coordinatewise linear inequalities in the period count. Check the finite prefix separately. A translating triple can miss the remote unit in higher dimensions. If it does, this is a terminal independent profile. If it hits, the full configuration has diameter at most $2B+2S$.

Thus an arbitrarily long trip of a compact triple either ends in a bounded full encounter or remains independent forever. It cannot carry two independently changing unbounded gaps through a continuing interaction. This includes triples that detach and recombine during their periodic orbit: their entire period, not just a section shape, is retained in the compact profile.

\section{Exact contact rays}\label{smc:zd:sec:rays}
At an emission checkpoint put the source residual unit at $0$ and write the dimer profile as
\[
 Q(r+kp)=kD+Q_r,\qquad D\ne0,\quad 0\le r<p,\quad k\in\NN.
\]
It never meets its source again in this isolated profile. For a target unit at $v$, contact at time $r+pk$ occurs exactly when
\begin{equation}\label{smc:zd:eq:ray}
 v=a+kD,\qquad a\in Q_r+([-2S,2S]^d\cap\Z^d).
\end{equation}
The notation denotes the union over the actual occupied sites of $Q_r$, with the phase $r$ retained. All offsets $a$ form a finite effective set. Repeated witnesses are harmless. Minimize the \emph{actual integer time} $r+pk$ over all valid witnesses. If there is no witness with $k\ge0$, the packet and both markers remain independent forever.

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=.78,>=Stealth]
\draw[step=1,gray!18,very thin] (-3,-3) grid (4,4);
\draw[blue!65!black,thick] (-2,-2) rectangle (2,2);
\draw[orange!80!black,thick] (-1,-1) rectangle (3,3);
\draw[black,dashed] (-2,-2) rectangle (3,3);
\fill (0,0) circle (3pt) node[below left] {$q_1$};
\fill (1,1) circle (3pt) node[below right] {$q_2$};
\fill[red!70!black] (-2,3) circle (3pt) node[above left] {$v$};
\node[align=center] at (.5,-3.85) {Actual contact boxes\\leave holes in their bounding hull};
\begin{scope}[xshift=10cm]
\draw[->,thick] (-2,0)--(3.6,0) node[right] {$D$};
\draw[->,thick,blue!65!black] (-1,-1)--(3,2) node[right] {$D'$};
\foreach \x in {-1,0,1,2,3} {\fill (\x,0) circle (2pt);}
\foreach \a in {0,.25,.5,.75,1} {\fill[blue!65!black] ({-1+4*\a},{-1+3*\a}) circle (2pt);}
\draw[dashed] (.333,0) circle (.5);
\node[align=center] at (.8,-3.85) {Nonparallel flight equations\\have at most one pair of counts};
\end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Left: for $S=1$, sites $(0,0),(1,1)$ do not contact $(-2,3)$, although their expanded bounding hull contains it. Right: schematic arithmetic rays; the finite exceptional region is computed from exact determinants, not from the drawing's angle or scale.}\label{smc:zd:fig:geometry}
\end{figure}

To express the successful rays in finite control, write $D=m\nu$, where $\nu$ is the primitive integer vector on its unoriented line, with first nonzero coordinate positive, and $m\in\Z\setminus\{0\}$. By Bezout's identity compute an integer linear functional $\lambda$ with $\lambda(\nu)=1$. Every vector has the unique decomposition
\[
 v=n\nu+z,\qquad n=\lambda(v),\qquad z=v-\lambda(v)\nu,\qquad\lambda(z)=0.
\]
For each contact offset define $\ell=\lambda(a)$ and $z_a=a-\ell\nu$. Equation~\eqref{smc:zd:eq:ray} becomes
\begin{align}
 z&=z_a,& n&\equiv\ell\pmod{|m|},& k&=(n-\ell)/m\ge0,\label{smc:zd:eq:residue}\\
 t&=(p/m)n+r-(p/m)\ell.&&&&\label{smc:zd:eq:affinetime}
\end{align}
For $|n|>\max_a|\lambda(a)|$ on the drift facing sign, all eligible counts are nonnegative. On the opposite sign there is no candidate. On the eligible sign, the transverse offset, residue, and phase identify the possible witnesses and the earliest constant term in~\eqref{smc:zd:eq:affinetime}.

Relative to the target, the entire contact dimer shape is $Q_r-a$. Hence its tagged seed is also determined by these finite data. If several witnesses attain the same actual time they describe the same complete configuration; a fixed witness ordering removes bookkeeping ambiguity. All intermediate flight phases remain available: their coordinates are affine in the marker vector and one period variable, with a linear first contact cutoff. Every local encounter prefix phase is retained too.

\section{The effective nonparallel exception}\label{smc:zd:sec:exception}
Let a dimer fly from marker $A$ to marker $B$. At the next emission the contacted marker is $B+c$, with $c$ in a finite library of marker update vectors, while the old source $A$ remains stationary. Thus the incoming marker vector $v=B-A$ and outgoing target vector $v'$ satisfy
\[
 v'=A-(B+c)=-v-c.
\]
If both flights succeed, with nonzero drifts $D,D'$, their contact ray equations give
\begin{equation}\label{smc:zd:eq:switch}
 v=a+kD,\quad v'=a'+k'D',\quad
 kD+k'D'=-(a+a'+c),\qquad k,k'\in\NN.
\end{equation}
The offsets $a,a',c$ range over finite computable lists.

\begin{lemma}[Finite nonparallel switch set]\label{smc:zd:lem:exception}
For all nonparallel pairs of emission drifts, the marker vectors in~\eqref{smc:zd:eq:switch} form an effectively computable finite set.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Choose coordinates $i,j$ with
\[
 \Delta_{ij}=D_iD'_j-D_jD'_i\ne0,
 \qquad H=-(a+a'+c).
\]
The only possible rational solution of the two selected equations is
\begin{equation}\label{smc:zd:eq:cramer}
 k=\frac{H_iD'_j-H_jD'_i}{\Delta_{ij}},\qquad
 k'=\frac{D_iH_j-D_jH_i}{\Delta_{ij}}.
\end{equation}
Retain it only when both entries are nonnegative integers and every remaining coordinate equation in~\eqref{smc:zd:eq:switch} holds. Compute $v=a+kD$. There are finitely many drift and offset inputs, so this enumerates the full finite exceptional set.
\end{proof}

This is an exact calculation, rather than a qualitative appeal to divergent directions. In particular, a bound depending only on the small offsets is insufficient. Let $M=10^6$ and set
\[
 D=(M,1),\quad D'=(-M-1,-1),\quad a=a'=0,\quad c=(0,-1).
\]
The determinant is $1$, while~\eqref{smc:zd:eq:switch} has the solution $k=M+1$, $k'=M$. The exceptional vector $v=(M+1)D$ has
\[
 \inorm v=M^2+M=1\,000\,001\,000\,000.
\]
Higher dimensional examples follow by adjoining zero coordinates. This shows why nearly parallel drift vectors can create a very large but finite exception.

Outside a computable bound every continuing sequence of successful dimer exchanges uses one fixed \emph{unoriented} rational line. Opposite orientations on that line are permitted. A nonparallel outgoing packet that misses the old marker is terminal, not another counter mode. A compact triple is already covered by Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:compact}.

\section{Finite control and one scalar gap}\label{smc:zd:sec:counter}
For every primitive line $\nu$ in the emission library, choose one Bezout functional $\lambda$. Order the two markers by their $\lambda$ coordinates, calling them $L$ and $R$, and write
\begin{equation}\label{smc:zd:eq:gap}
 R-L=x\nu+z,\qquad x=\lambda(R-L)>0,\qquad\lambda(z)=0.
\end{equation}
This is a bookkeeping order for the chosen line, not a global linear order of $\Z^d$. For a successful long flight the transverse offset belongs to the finite set of $\pm(a-\lambda(a)\nu)$ from contact offsets on this line. Its sign depends on the source side.

The finite mode records $\nu$, the source side, the complete emission profile and launch shape, the transverse offset, and any needed tagged seed information. The absolute vector anchor $L$ is retained as output; it does not affect the next rule application.

\subsection{Noncircular threshold selection}\label{smc:zd:sec:thresholds}
\wtag\ [At $d=1$ these are source~17's thresholds~\eqref{smc:fm:eq:thresholds}, $N=4B+12S$ and $W=N+2B$, also chosen in that order; here $N$ must in addition exceed the Bezout and exceptional-switch quantities.]
Choose an integer $N$ larger than all required finite quantities:
\begin{enumerate}
\item every $|\lambda(a)|$ in the contact lists, to validate the large gap residue formulas;
\item every $|\lambda(c)|$ for marker shifts, so the marker order cannot reverse;
\item every $|\lambda(v)|$ for exceptional switch vectors, for the relevant incoming functionals;
\item a safety bound separating the remote marker from every local seed prefix.
\end{enumerate}
For the fourth item it suffices, with further finite launch offsets included if needed, to take
\[
 N>\max_{\lambda}\lVert\lambda\rVert_1(B+10S+1),
\]
since $|\lambda(v)|\le\lVert\lambda\rVert_1\inorm v$. In particular $x>N$ makes the remote marker safely farther than $B+6S$ from the incoming seed anchor. All data used here were already computed from the original seed and phase libraries.

Now choose $W$ larger than $6S$, $B+6S$, $2B+2S$, and the diameter of every successful emission launch with $0<x\le N$. This last list is finite: the lines and transverse offsets are finite, the scalar ranges over a finite interval, and all launch shapes have fixed finite offsets. More explicitly, a successful vector satisfies $n=\lambda(a)+km$ with $k\ge0$. If $|n|\le N$, then
\[
 0\le k\le\frac{N+|\lambda(a)|}{|m|}.
\]
Enumerate those finitely many integers for every offset and profile, and include their full launch diameters in $W$. This also includes $n=0$ and every degenerate marker order. Thus $W$ is computed \emph{after} $N$, without enlarging and recomputing the seed library.

Call a configuration of diameter at most $W$ ordinary. At designated emission checkpoints use the live representation when the next cross marker flight succeeds and $x>N$. Otherwise use an ordinary state if its diameter is at most $W$, or an explicit terminal independent profile if the flight misses. At other dispatch states test ordinary first. Ordinary and tagged live states may overlap; the dispatch convention is part of the algorithm, not an extra state of the CA.

\subsection{The transition formula}
On a live to live edge Lemma~\ref{smc:zd:lem:exception} prevents a nonparallel line switch. Only the contacted marker changes, by one vector $c$. Since the order remains fixed,
\[
 x'=x+c_0,\qquad L'=L+e,\qquad z'=z+c_\perp,
\]
with constants from finite effective lists. Fold $z$ into the mode. Let $M_0$ be the least common multiple of the positive numbers $|m|$ in all emission drift decompositions. Equations~\eqref{smc:zd:eq:residue}--\eqref{smc:zd:eq:affinetime}, plus the fixed local prefix duration, give
\begin{equation}\label{smc:zd:eq:machine}
 q'=f(q,x\bmod M_0),\quad x'=x+c_0,\quad
 L'=L+e,\quad h=\alpha x+\beta,
\end{equation}
where $\alpha>0$ is rational and $h$ is a positive integer on the permitted residue. Here $\alpha=p/|m|$ for the flight. Positivity follows because live targets are safely distant.
\wtag\ [At $d=1$ this is source~17's Lemma~\ref{smc:fm:lem:counter}, equation~\eqref{smc:fm:eq:counter}, with the marker gap $D$ in the role of the scalar $x$.]

An updated successful gap at most $N$ has bounded full diameter and resets to ordinary. An emission that misses is terminal. A compact outcome is analyzed by Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:compact}; it then resets or is terminal. The \emph{choice to continue live or exit through a compact outcome or emission miss}, apart from bounded lower guard effects, depends only on mode and residue. Whether a compact outcome subsequently hits the remote marker may depend on the actual gap, and is calculated separately. It is not a live edge and must not be treated as residue determined.

There is no further control clock: the CA is time independent, the units are stationary in $G$, and packet phase is already recorded. Neither absolute anchor nor accumulated time is queried by the control. The vector separation is consequently not a pair of freely testable scalar counters.

\section{Complete dispatch from arbitrary inputs}\label{smc:zd:sec:dispatch}
For total mass four the possible $2S$ component mass partitions are
\[
 4,\qquad 3+1,\qquad 2+2,\qquad 2+1+1,\qquad 1+1+1+1.
\]
Lower masses were covered above. Each mass partition is handled as follows. \wtag\ [At $d=1$ the same five partitions are source~17's Lemma~\ref{smc:fm:lem:dispatch}.]

\begin{description}[style=nextline,leftmargin=0pt,labelsep=0pt]
\item[Connected mass four] Its diameter is at most $6S$, so it is ordinary. This includes a weight four singleton and all weighted connected shapes.
\item[Four unit components] All are stationary.
\item[Mass three plus one] Outside ordinary, the connected triple has diameter at most $4S$. Since $W>B+6S$, the remote unit is far enough from the chosen triple seed anchor for the library prefix to be independent. Apply the tagged seed library and Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:compact}, or enter an emission checkpoint.
\item[Two dimers] Combine their finite transients and then take a common phase period. At each common phase the relative occupied coordinates are affine in one nonnegative integer period count. Their first distance at most $2S$ event is the earliest solution among finitely many coordinatewise interval tests, one for each pair of occupied sites. If absent, the tail is independent. At contact, each packet has diameter at most $2S$, so the full configuration has diameter at most $6S$ and is ordinary.
\item[A dimer and two units] Use the exact contact calculation against each unit, including any finite dimer transient. If the initial dimer has eventual drift $D=0$, inspect its transient and one entire positioned period against both units; the nonzero drift ray formula is unnecessary. If neither is hit, the tail is independent. A first simultaneous contact with both yields a connected mass four state of diameter at most $6S$. Otherwise the contact forms a tagged triple seed. If the full state is ordinary, reset. If not, the remaining unit satisfies the safety separation and the library applies.
\end{description}

Actual times are compared across all sites, phases, and possible target units. Ties are retained at the level of complete configurations. No argument assumes a packet must hit a marker merely because one coordinate moves toward it.

From an ordinary state take at least one exact CA step before invoking dispatch. This yields a positive time edge from one of finitely many normalized ordinary states to another ordinary state, a live checkpoint, or an explicit terminal profile. All intervening configurations are kept as finite phase descriptions. A live edge has a positive length flight. Thus the section graph has no artificial zero time loops.

\section{Termination and the complete orbit form}\label{smc:zd:sec:termination}
Enlarge the finite mode by $x\bmod M_0$. Follow the section machine from the given input. A repeat of a normalized ordinary state means two complete configurations are translates, so the whole future is periodic up to translation, at every intermediate time.

Within an uninterrupted live excursion, the unoriented line is fixed and there are finitely many enlarged modes. At a repeated mode let $\Delta$ be the net scalar change around the recorded cycle. The residue has repeated, hence $\Delta$ is divisible by $M_0$ and the same residue itinerary is available on later repetitions.
\begin{description}[style=nextline,leftmargin=0pt,labelsep=0pt]
\item[$\Delta=0$] The whole launch configuration repeats up to the anchor translation. Determinism gives a translated periodic future.
\item[$\Delta>0$] Every intermediate lower guard valid in the recorded cycle remains valid in all later repetitions. The same itinerary persists forever, giving an expanding shuttle.
\item[$\Delta<0$] Compute the greatest number of complete repetitions for which every intermediate scalar stays above $N$. This is a finite collection of linear inequalities. Execute or accelerate those repetitions. The remaining finite cycle prefix exits the live region to ordinary or terminal. An above threshold mode dependent exit would already have appeared during the recorded cycle, because the enlarged control itinerary is the same.
\end{description}
The decreasing case can be simulated without acceleration: there are finitely many repetitions, however large that number is. Acceleration merely gives a compact exact description of the finite prefix.

There are only finitely many normalized ordinary states. Therefore the analysis terminates with a finite prefix and one of three eventual forms:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\Alph*.]
\item independent finite phase objects;
\item the entire configuration periodic up to translation;
\item an expanding shuttle.
\end{enumerate}
All described prefixes and phases are effective. This is an unbounded termination proof, not an inference from finite experiments.
\wtag\ [At $d=1$ this is source~17's Proposition~\ref{smc:fm:prop:normal}, with the same three cases for $\Delta$.]

\subsection{Expanding shuttle coordinates and time}\label{smc:zd:sec:shuttle}
Suppose the cycle in case C has $\Delta>0$ and total anchor update $E\in\Z^d$. At its corresponding sections,
\begin{equation}\label{smc:zd:eq:cycles}
 x_n=x_0+n\Delta,\qquad L_n=L_0+nE,\qquad n\in\NN.
\end{equation}
There are finitely many edges and local phases per cycle. During a local encounter prefix every occupied coordinate is affine in $n$. During a flight it is affine in $(n,j)$, where $j\in\NN$ is the packet period count subject to linear first contact bounds. Labels are fixed within a phase. These descriptions include the whole orbit between sections. Their finite union is Presburger definable when elapsed time is forgotten.

The duration of every live edge is $\alpha x+\beta$. Its duration at corresponding cycles grows by $\alpha\Delta>0$. Summing finitely many edges gives a positive affine cycle duration, so section times have the form
\begin{equation}\label{smc:zd:eq:quadratic}
 T_n=T_0+A\frac{n(n-1)}2+Cn,\qquad A>0.
\end{equation}
The effective rational coefficients take integral values on all natural cycle indices. The duration of cycle $n$ is $An+C>0$.

There are effective constants $K_0,K_1\ge0$ such that every stationary frame occupied point $y$ in cycle $n$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{smc:zd:eq:linearbound}
 \inorm y\le K_0+K_1n.
\end{equation}
Both markers are affine in $n$; every local offset is bounded; the flight period count is bounded above by an affine function of $n$; and every packet drift and phase offset is fixed finite data. Taking maxima over finitely many phases yields the bound. This reasoning requires neither coordinatewise monotonicity nor a line through the spatial origin.

\section{Deciding observations and restoring translation}\label{smc:zd:sec:observations}
In cases A and B, a common period expresses all occupied coordinates and time affinely in a nonnegative integer, with finitely many phases and a finite transient. Exact targets and finite patterns are finite Presburger predicates on these coordinates. For example, a prescribed nonzero site must equal some occupied site with the prescribed label; a prescribed zero site must differ from every occupied site. There are at most four occupied sites and finitely many label possibilities. Exact configuration equality also requires that there be no additional occupied sites. Quantify an integer vector for translated observations. Restoring~\eqref{smc:zd:eq:restore} preserves affine formulas here.

In case C, the finite union of the affine in $(n,j)$ stationary frame phase formulas decides every \emph{untimed} exact target and pattern query for $G$, anchored or translated. Translation invariant queries have the same answer for $F$ and $G$ by~\eqref{smc:zd:eq:restore}. When $\delta=0$, this finishes all queries.

Suppose $\delta\ne0$ and consider an anchored query for $F$. Choose a coordinate $i$ with $\delta_i\ne0$. Since $t\ge T_n$ throughout cycle $n$, equations~\eqref{smc:zd:eq:restore} and~\eqref{smc:zd:eq:linearbound} imply
\begin{align}
 y_i(F^t(c))&\ge\delta_iT_n-K_0-K_1n&&\text{if }\delta_i>0,\label{smc:zd:eq:cutpos}\\
 y_i(F^t(c))&\le\delta_iT_n+K_0+K_1n&&\text{if }\delta_i<0.\label{smc:zd:eq:cutneg}
\end{align}
The reversed inequality in the second line is essential. The nonzero quadratic leading term eventually dominates the linear term uniformly over every phase and every time in the cycle.
\wtag\ [At $d=1$ this cutoff is source~17's Section~\ref{smc:fm:sec:anchored}.]

For any specified finite target window, compute $n_*$ beyond which the appropriate bound lies permanently beyond that window. One may solve a rational quadratic inequality, or increase $n$ until the bound is beyond the window and its forward difference points outward; discrete convexity then proves permanence. Consequently a nonempty exact target, or an anchored pattern prescribing any nonzero site, can occur only before $T_{n_*}$. That finite time interval can simply be simulated. Its possible enormous size does not affect decidability. A wholly zero anchored pattern occurs after the cutoff. An empty exact configuration is settled by mass conservation; mass zero input is handled immediately.

This completes the proof of Theorem~\ref{smc:zd:thm:main} when a unit symbol exists. In particular, the proof restores absolute position without invoking unrestricted quadratic Diophantine solvability and without introducing an absolute position control counter.

\section{Alphabets with no unit symbol}\label{smc:zd:sec:nounit}
When no symbol has weight one, mass at most four occupies at most two sites. A singleton of weight two or three cannot split into two nonvacuum sites, so it is a finite state walker. Its label evolution and vector increments become periodic. A weight four singleton may split into two weight two walkers; at the split their separation is at most $2R$.

Take a finite normalized core of singleton and two site configurations of diameter at most $2\max(1,R)$. Outside it the only nontrivial case is two independent weight two walkers. Their finite transients and common eventual phases give coordinatewise affine inequalities for the first core encounter. Solve them exactly, or certify no encounter. A core return has a computable finite elapsed time and translation. Its finite core return graph gives an eventually translated periodic whole configuration or a terminal pair of independent walkers, exactly as in Lemma~\ref{smc:zd:lem:triple}. These profiles have timed Presburger descriptions and decide all four observations.

Symbols of weight greater than four never appear at these total masses, by conservation and positivity. This also covers every missing small mass and weighted singleton case, completing the theorem.

\section{The effective decision algorithm}\label{smc:zd:sec:algorithm}
The construction may be implemented eagerly or by computing only visited normalized states. It uses the promised rule hypotheses as follows.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Check mass zero and immediate label or mass impossibilities. If there is no unit symbol, use Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:nounit}.
\item Compute the isolated unit displacement $\delta$, the stationary unit rule $G$, and $S$.
\item Enumerate normalized mass two states of diameter at most $2S$ and compute their finite profiles.
\item Enumerate normalized mass three states of diameter at most $4S$, build their return graph, and compute the tagged connected seed library, its prefixes, compact and emission profiles, and $B$.
\item Enumerate actual site contact offsets and phases. Compute primitive drift directions and Bezout functionals.
\item Enumerate marker shifts and solve~\eqref{smc:zd:eq:switch} for every nonparallel profile and offset combination, retaining only valid nonnegative integral solutions in all coordinates.
\item Select $N$ and then $W$ as in Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:thresholds}. Construct ordinary dispatch and finite live mode residue transitions as needed.
\item Follow Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:termination} until case A, B, or C is established, retaining exact durations, vector anchors, and all intermediate phases.
\item Test the finite prefix and eventual profiles using Section~\ref{smc:zd:sec:observations}, with the effective original frame cutoff when necessary.
\end{enumerate}
Every enumeration is finite. Unbounded event searches reduce to one variable integer linear equations or inequalities, or to the proved one counter termination argument. Presburger decision is applied only after the explicit finite formulas have been constructed. No step replaces an unbounded question by an unjustified finite search.

\section{The inherited mass five upper bound}\label{smc:zd:sec:boundary}
Let $H$ be the previously established one dimensional binary five particle universal CA. For $x=(x_1,\ldots,x_d)$, define $\widehat H$ by applying $H$ independently on each line parallel to the first coordinate axis. It has the same finite alphabet and radius bound when the one dimensional neighborhood is regarded as a subset of $\Z^d$. A finite configuration meets finitely many such lines, and conservation on each line gives conservation of the finite total particle count.

If $H$ is reversible, apply $H^{-1}$ independently on the same lines. This is the inverse of $\widehat H$. If $H=I_2\circ I_1$ with full shift involutions $I_1,I_2$, their linewise lifts remain involutions on the entire higher dimensional full shift, and preserve finite particle number when the original involutions do. An input supported on a single line has exactly its original one dimensional dynamics on that line; all other lines remain vacuum.

Therefore every effective input encoding, clean target or halt observation, exact clock, and source simulation proved for the chosen one dimensional construction is inherited on a fixed line. Reports 16 and 26 \textup{[batch~82; Report~16 is Part~II of \path{five-particle-binary-automata}, Report~26 is placed there; note after Corollary~\ref{smc:zd:cor:boundary}]} supply the relevant universal source and compilation statements. This paragraph does not independently reconstruct that source or materialize a universal rule array. Together with Theorem~\ref{smc:zd:thm:main}, it gives Corollary~\ref{smc:zd:cor:boundary} in that inherited simulation setting.

Report 12's \textup{[source~17's, Proposition~\ref{smc:fm:prop:hits}]} binary four particle quadratic shuttle also embeds linewise. Hence decidability at four does \emph{not} imply eventual timed semilinearity. Report 13 \textup{[source~18, Part~\ref{smc:su:part}]} already treats fixed input timed quartic certificates in the one dimensional setting; it is a precursor, not a result reclassified here as a discovery.

The hypotheses delimit the conclusion. Multiple unit labels can give independent finite state walkers with different drifts, invalidating the stationary dimer argument. Negative weights, weight zero nonvacuum symbols, infinite alphabets, active vacuum, infinite input scenery, and time or position dependent rules are outside the theorem. Infinite marked axes or a free external tape cannot be silently supplied without changing the model.

\section{Relationship to earlier literature}\label{smc:zd:sec:literature}
The literature check included close pebble and walker models as well as conservative cellular automata. It did not locate a primary theorem deciding reachability for the exact class in Theorem~\ref{smc:zd:thm:main}. That bounded search result supplies no novelty or priority conclusion. The accompanying literature record separates inspected full texts from publisher abstracts.

Delorme and Mazoyer study pebble automata on the unbounded plane and report a three pebble intrinsic universality threshold, with no intrinsically universal two pebble automaton~\cite{delorme2002}. Their publisher abstract was checked, but the full 2002 article was not retrieved. Their finite state head and passive movable pebbles are a close predecessor model. Intrinsic universality is distinct from the exact reachability question here. A naive unique unit encoding charges at least mass two for a stateful head and one for each pebble; this bookkeeping observation is an inference, not their theorem or an established complete encoding.

Lacalle and Gajardo analyze two distinguishable pebbles on a one symbol grid~\cite{lacalle2014}. Their full preprint provides one pebble periodicity, geometric ray escape arguments, and real time two counter recognition of two pebble traces. Its conclusion explicitly extends its geometric argument to $\Z^d$. Trace recognition by a counter machine is not itself a reachability decision theorem. The present proof must additionally account for CA cluster splitting, fusion, control transfer, weighted labels, and synchronous intermediate phases. The transient bound in their local one pebble argument should not be applied to an arbitrary distant head and pebble start.

Blum and Sakoda report, with attribution to Sipser, that two planar finite automata and one pebble simulate a universal two counter machine~\cite{blum1977}. This cautions against equating agent count with conserved mass: two independently stateful agents and a pebble have naive minimum unique unit mass $2+2+1=5$. Their discussion of marked axes likewise cannot provide a free finite mass background. Their two pebble nonuniversality statement is not substituted for our decision algorithm.

Salo and T\"orm\"a's plane walking model has independently controlled heads reading a separate supplied configuration~\cite{salo2014}. Its sparse input and hierarchy results are relevant, but static marks carry information and must be counted in a conservative encoding. Independently controlled heads cannot automatically be represented by interchangeable weight one symbols.

Morita, Tojima, Imai, and Ogiro give genuinely two dimensional reversible number conserving universal constructions~\cite{morita2002}. The accessible publisher abstract does not establish the present mass four or unique unit restriction. Schaeffer's number conserving physically universal CA has a diffusion theorem bounding the duration of finite configuration computation~\cite{schaeffer2014}; it is not a small finite seed Turing universality counterexample. Block phases and velocity tracks also need explicit encoding when comparing its model to an ordinary translation invariant CA.

Kong's thesis discusses the one dimensional binary small particle hierarchy and leaves four particles open in its concluding discussion~\cite{kongthesis}. It neither proves the weighted higher dimensional theorem nor explicitly gives this uniform reachability procedure. The separately identifiable particle complexity paper is Alhazov and Imai~\cite{alhazovimai}; its full construction was not independently reverified in this literature pass. For the inherited upper bound, the supplied Reports 16 and 26 \textup{[batch~82; Part~II of, and placed in, \path{five-particle-binary-automata}]} remain the actual proof dependencies.

\section{Executable evidence and reproducibility}\label{smc:zd:sec:repro}
The package includes the unchanged arithmetic audit source and its deterministic expected receipt. It tests the formulas used in the proof, not the class of all CA rules. Normal and optimized Python runs have the same exactly typed receipt. The audit uses explicit failure checks, not optimization removable assertions.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{p{.64\linewidth}r}
\toprule
Finite check & Count \\
\midrule
First contact profiles in dimensions 1 to 3 & 600 \\
Exact contact ray shifts exceeding $10^{60}$ & 600 \\
Tied earliest contact witness cases & 30 \\
Common clock two packet comparisons in dimensions 1 to 5 & 500 \\
Nonparallel switch equations in dimensions 2 to 6 & 500 \\
Two unit intersection box checks in dimensions 1 to 4 & 892 \\
Original frame escape cutoffs & 1,000 \\
Expanded hull false positive regression & 1 \\
Nearly parallel exceptional separation & $1\,000\,001\,000\,000$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The final row is a norm, not a number of trials. Randomized finite cases use the fixed seed $20261003$. Bounded direct traces are compared only inside their tested windows; they are not used to infer nonoccurrence beyond those windows. Huge ray shifts are tested by exact integer arithmetic.

The replay entrypoint verifies an independently fixed required inventory and the pinned scientific source digest before running any packaged Python helper. The full manifest checks every payload byte and rejects extra files and symlinks. Tests execute in fresh external temporary copies, in both normal and optimized modes. Their receipts are checked recursively with exact value \emph{and type} equality against the expected schema, so booleans cannot masquerade as integer counts. The original scientific payload is checked before and after for identical bytes, permission modes, and modification times. Outputs are written outside the release directory.

A tamper regression exercises changed and resealed scientific code, missing or extra payloads, and symlink substitution. This is accidental corruption and dependency substitution protection around a trusted entrypoint, not cryptographic authentication against an adversary who replaces the entire package and all trust anchors. The ZIP hash must be obtained from a trusted distribution if authenticity matters.

Run \file{./reproduce.sh --output-dir ../report29-replay} from the extracted release, after inspecting the script if desired. Run \file{./build.sh ../report29-build} to rebuild the PDF into an external directory using pdfLaTeX. The README gives dependencies, scope, and exact output semantics. Copyrighted literature reading copies are excluded.

A fresh independent arithmetic audit additionally checks 4,335 exact contact comparisons (including zero drift), 2,172 finite control seed and time invariances, 7,040 nonparallel solution recoveries, 58,800 small launch bounds, and 28,800 indices beyond quadratic cutoffs. It also rejects a remaining coordinate inconsistency and a bounding hull false positive, and exercises a distinct nearly parallel exception of norm $1\,000\,002\,000\,000$. Its delivered copy changes only the proof binding hash to that of the sanitized proof copy; the arithmetic source and expected values are otherwise unchanged.

The proof was independently inspected as ordinary mathematics. The arithmetic evidence strengthens confidence in the contact and cutoff calculations; it does not constitute a formal proof, a general rule to section compiler, or a complete end to end simulation test for every promised input.

Not shipped: the manuscript (printed here), its PDF, README and checksum ledger. The replay entry point checks an exact inventory and pinned hashes under the delivered names, so it runs only in the delivered layout (README). At placement on Windows, \path{replay.py} passed in normal and optimized mode (receipts typed-equal to the expected ones) and \path{tamper_regression.py} rejected every tamper case.

\wtag\ \textbf{The programme's review.} The Hilbert's-tenth programme reviewed this archive's placed proof in \path{review_dimension_independent_four_mass.md}, with its checker and receipt, in \path{Computability/HilbertTenthProblem/Papers/research-wip/native-stream-queue/} (commit \texttt{7b547f686}, 3 October 2026)~\cite{reviewzd}: the proof ``passes this mathematical review''. It read the complete proof, including the no-unit case and the absolute-frame observation cutoff, and a separate reviewer of the proof and the new checker found no gap. Its new checks are arithmetic fixtures, not a cellular-automaton analyzer: support contact against 216 targets of a three-dimensional packet profile, 125 ray translations, the residue and transverse decomposition, a marker missed despite movement toward it, six nonparallel pairs on 1,470 right sides against a bounded search (with a parallel opposing pair rejected), and three counter cycles. It executed none of the archive's Python. It states that the hypotheses are necessary to the argument, that the five-particle upper bound is inherited from separate source and compiler proofs which it does not reprove, and that no arithmetic-circuit lower bound or smaller universal polynomial follows.

\section{Further questions}\label{smc:zd:sec:questions}
Several next steps would add information without changing the present theorem.
\begin{enumerate}
\item\label{smc:zd:q:pebble} Give a precise comparison theorem between the CA encounter normal form and classical two pebble machines. The reduction must preserve splitting, fusion, geometric tags, and observation semantics, rather than merely count objects.
\item\label{smc:zd:q:compiler} Implement and verify the finite rule to encounter library compiler for bounded small rules. The present arithmetic audit does not implement that construction. A small verified compiler would test the integration points between exact local evolution, first contacts, and section dispatch.
\item\label{smc:zd:q:size} Quantify state library sizes, determinant exceptions, and Presburger formula growth as functions of dimension, radius, alphabet, and weights. The proof makes no complexity claim; the nearly parallel example excludes especially naive small geometric bounds.
\item\label{smc:zd:q:units} Classify which relaxations of unit uniqueness already allow undecidable fixed mass dynamics. Multiple labels can provide independent walker control, but that observation alone is not a complete conservative CA construction at a specified mass. (\wtag\ Added 3 October 2026: open. In $d=1$ Part~V shows that many weight-one types already allow undecidable pattern occurrence at mass three (Theorem~\ref{smc:tm:thm:threshold}); the least relaxation is open, as source~17's Question~\ref{smc:fm:q:severalunits} also says.)
\item\label{smc:zd:q:quartic} Optimize the input dependent canonical timed certificates of Appendix~\ref{smc:zd:app:quartic}, particularly the spatial sorting and fixed pattern refinement costs, without confusing those bounds with a uniform arity theorem over arbitrary inputs. (\wtag\ Added 3 October 2026: open. Source~18's selector elimination (Section~\ref{smc:tq:sec:compiler}) saves one witness per chart family, and its four-witness binary certificate (Section~\ref{smc:tq:sec:fourwitness}) shows what shared parameters can save in one explicit family; neither is a general optimization.)
\item\label{smc:zd:q:formal} Formalize the dimer invariance, first contact arithmetic, nonparallel exception, and section termination lemmas in a proof assistant. The currently supplied evidence is mathematical exposition and finite executable checking.
\end{enumerate}


\bigskip
\phantomsection\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Source 20: Sparse orbit geometry and exact counting}
\noindent{\large\bfseries Source 20 (batch-82 manuscript 17): Sparse orbit geometry and exact counting}\par
\noindent\emph{Semilinear spatial sets, quadratic clocks, and the effects of drift}, ``Research report 30'', 3 October 2026. \wtag\ From the archive \path{Sparse_Orbit_Geometry_and_Exact_Counting_Package.zip}; its labels carry the prefix \texttt{smc:og:}, its sections are Sections~\ref{smc:og:sec:scope}--\ref{smc:og:sec:reproduction} here, and its appendix is Appendix~\ref{smc:og:app:quartic}, after source~19's. It builds on source~19 (``Report~29''), which it pins by SHA-256. The Hilbert's-tenth programme made a scoped intake of its original-frame certificate (note in Appendix~\ref{smc:og:app:quartic}). Its abstract follows; the bracketed note in it is \wtag.
\begin{quote}\small
For a fixed finite input of conserved mass at most four, the orbit classification in Report 29 [source~19] has quantitative geometric consequences. In the frame fixing the unique possible unit symbol, the set of distinct occupied sites is effectively semilinear and lies in finitely many translates of one rational subspace of rank at most two. Centered box counts are eventually quasipolynomial of degree zero, one, or two. An expanding shuttle has sharp degree $r=\rank_{\Q}\{E,\nu\}$, discovery count $\alpha c^{-r/2}T^{r/2}+O(T^{(r-1)/2})$, and first arrival times piecewise rational quadratic in the site coordinates. We retain the exact quadratic clock and its floor oscillations, prove inverse estimates, and give a stronger exact rank one inverse theorem. A fully specified radius six weighted cellular automaton realizes a planar wedge. Restoring a nonzero unit drift can destroy both semilinearity and eventual box quasipolynomiality: the same example has an exact floor root count $3N-3\sqrt N+O(1)$. A third direction is genuinely needed for another drifted realization, even against finite unions of bounded width planes. A constructive appendix gives ordinary degree at most four Diophantine certificates with exactly one natural witness tuple for each stationary frame visited site and each reachable canonical complete configuration in either frame. The stationary first time uses one shared cycle variable; full original frame targets use whole orbit injectivity. The mathematical proofs are separated from finite computational checks. The report uses existing Presburger counting theorems and states its close relationship to classical two pebble work; no novelty, binary realization, or reversibility claim is made.
\end{quote}

\section{Scope and the inherited classification}\label{smc:og:sec:scope}
Let $F$ be a deterministic, translation equivariant, finite radius cellular automaton on $\Z^d$, for a finite $d\ge1$. Its finite alphabet $A$ has a unique weight zero vacuum $0$, positive integer weights on every other symbol, and at most one symbol $u$ of weight one. Assume that $F$ preserves the total weight of every finite configuration. Fix a finite input $c_0$ of mass at most four. The vacuum is quiescent. These are exactly the promise hypotheses of the lower mass classification in Report 29~\textup{[source~19, Part~\ref{smc:zd:part}]}.

If $u$ exists, its isolated evolution is a single unit translated by a fixed vector $\delta\in\Z^d$. With the convention $\tau_v c(x)=c(x-v)$, put
\begin{equation}\label{smc:og:eq:normalization}
 G=\tau_{-\delta}F,\qquad F^t=\tau_{t\delta}G^t.
\end{equation}
Then $G$ fixes isolated units. If no unit symbol exists, use $G=F$ and $\delta=0$. All unlabeled counts below count the union of occupied sites, regardless of the state occupying a site on a particular visit.

\begin{definition}
The stationary frame spatial set, centered box count, and time discovery count are
\begin{align*}
 S&=\bigcup_{t\ge0}\supp(G^t(c_0)),\\
 C(N)&=\#\bigl(S\cap[-N,N]^d\bigr),\qquad N\in\NN,\\
 V(T)&=\#\bigcup_{0\le t\le T}\supp(G^t(c_0)),\qquad T\in\NN.
\end{align*}
Each site contributes once. These are not counts of visits, occupied site and time pairs, or phase witnesses. Write $S_F$ and $C_F$ for the corresponding original frame quantities.
\end{definition}

\subsection{Exactly what is used from Report 29}
The present report does not repeat the decision theorem or the full proof that a sparse CA has the following classification. It uses the effective orbit construction of Report 29, Sections 2--12 \textup{[source~19, Sections~\ref{smc:zd:sec:frame}--\ref{smc:zd:sec:nounit}]}, particularly the fixed line reduction, additive scalar gap, and complete intermediate phase description. After a computable finite prefix, precisely one of the following forms is supplied:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\Alph*.]
\item Independent finite phase objects. Taking a common period, each occupied coordinate is one of finitely many $p+kD_i$, $k\in\NN$. In the stationary unit frame there are at most two nonzero drift directions: a genuinely mobile object costs mass at least two.
\item A whole configuration periodic up to translation. Its occupied coordinates are finitely many rays $p+kD$ with one common drift.
\item An expanding shuttle. A repeated finite control cycle has scalar gap, anchor, and exact section times
\begin{equation}\label{smc:og:eq:section}
 x_n=x_0+n\Delta,\quad L_n=L_0+nE,\quad
 T_n=T_0+cn^2+bn,
\end{equation}
where $\Delta>0$, $c>0$, and all data are effective. Section times are integral and strictly increasing; $b,c$ may be rational. A fixed primitive integer vector $\nu\ne0$ gives the common unoriented flight line. Every edge, encounter prefix, packet period phase, label, and within cycle time offset is retained.
\end{enumerate}
The source writes $T_n=T_0+A n(n-1)/2+Cn$, so here $c=A/2$ and $b=C-A/2$. This is a change of notation, not an extra timing assumption. The exact affine within cycle offsets used later also come from those retained phases. Knowledge only of an unordered spatial union and $T_n\sim cn^2$ would not suffice for the first arrival assertions.

The entire dependency is fixed input. ``Effective'' means that a finite description can be constructed from the promised rule and this input. It does not mean a small description, a polynomial time algorithm, an implemented general classifier, or a single uniform timed Presburger description covering arbitrary inputs.

\subsection{Main conclusions}
A rational affine plane is a translate of a real subspace spanned by integer vectors. Its rank is the dimension of that subspace. A set has finite width in such a plane if it is contained in the plane plus a fixed bounded set.

\begin{theorem}[Stationary frame geometry and counting]\label{smc:og:thm:geometry}
Under the stated hypotheses, the following descriptions are effective.
\begin{enumerate}
\item $S$ is semilinear, equivalently Presburger definable. Its characteristic generating function is rational after partitioning $\Z^d$ into disjoint sign orthants and reflecting each orthant into $\NN^d$.
\item $S$ lies in finitely many exact affine translates of one rational subspace of rank at most two, hence in a finite width rational affine plane of rank at most two.
\item For a nonempty orbit, $C(N)$ is eventually quasipolynomial of degree zero, one, or two. Degree zero is equivalent to $S$ being finite. If $S$ is infinite, then
\begin{equation}\label{smc:og:eq:Cgeneral}
 C(N)=\kappa N^r+O(N^{r-1}),\qquad \kappa\in\Q_{>0},\quad r\in\{1,2\}.
\end{equation}
\item Counts in boxes with independently variable integer lower and upper endpoints are piecewise quasipolynomial. Any fixed affine one parameter family of finite integer boxes consequently has an eventual quasipolynomial count of degree at most two, possibly zero or of smaller degree.
\end{enumerate}
For independent or translated periodic tails the degree is one if any occupied ray has nonzero drift, and zero otherwise. For an expanding shuttle it is exactly $r=\rank_\Q\{E,\nu\}$. The empty orbit has identically zero count.
\end{theorem}
A rank two containing plane does not force degree two: two nonparallel independent rays already need such a plane but have only linearly many sites in a box.

\begin{theorem}[Expanding shuttle timing]\label{smc:og:thm:timing}
For an expanding shuttle, let $D(K)$ count the distinct sites in the fixed prefix and the first $K$ complete eventual cycles. Use the half open cycle intervals $[T_n,T_{n+1})$. Then $D$ is effectively eventually quasipolynomial and
\begin{equation}\label{smc:og:eq:Dgeneral}
 D(K)=\alpha K^r+O(K^{r-1}),\qquad \alpha\in\Q_{>0},\quad r=\rank_\Q\{E,\nu\}.
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{smc:og:eq:Vgeneral}
 V(T)=\alpha c^{-r/2}T^{r/2}+O(T^{(r-1)/2}).
\end{equation}
The first visit time $\tau(y)$ on $S$ is effectively piecewise rational polynomial of degree at most two on a finite semilinear partition. For visited sites escaping every bounded set,
\begin{equation}\label{smc:og:eq:arrivalorder}
 \tau(y)=\Theta(\inorm y^2).
\end{equation}
The largest first visit time of a site in $S\cap[-N,N]^d$ is $\Theta(N^2)$.
\end{theorem}
The last assertion is coverage of the sites this particular orbit eventually visits, not coverage of the entire lattice box. The count $V(T)$ need not be eventually quasipolynomial in ordinary time.

\begin{theorem}[Drift restoration]\label{smc:og:thm:drift}
For an expanding shuttle, $S_F$ is contained in finitely many affine translates of $\operatorname{span}_{\R}\{E,\nu,\delta\}$, of rank at most three. If $\delta\ne0$, then $C_F(N)=\Theta(N)$. Nevertheless $S_F$ need not be semilinear and $C_F$ need not be eventually quasipolynomial. These failures and rank three sharpness occur for explicit finite range, globally weight conserving CAs with one unit symbol and a mass four input. Independent and whole translated periodic tails remain semilinear in the original frame.
\end{theorem}

\section{Complete phase coordinates and the common plane}\label{smc:og:sec:phase}
At edge $i$ of cycle $n$, the two geometrically designated markers have coordinates
\begin{align*}
 L_0+nE+e_i,\
 L_0+nE+e_i+(x_0+n\Delta+d_i)\nu+z_i.
\end{align*}
Here $e_i,d_i,z_i$ are fixed data in the finite mode. The symbols need not distinguish the markers: their names are bookkeeping roles. A packet emitted from either marker begins at a bounded offset from it and has a fixed period drift $m_i\nu$, with nonzero integer $m_i$. Its retained phase sites have the form
\begin{equation}\label{smc:og:eq:phase}
 y=p_i+n(E+\varepsilon_i\Delta\nu)+j m_i\nu,
 \qquad \varepsilon_i\in\{0,1\}.
\end{equation}
The integer domain is a finite conjunction or finite union of linear inequalities and congruences in $n,j$. After splitting finitely many residues and boundary phases, it has $0\le j\le a_i n+b_i$. Each genuine growing full flight phase has $a_i>0$. Constant offsets and initial gap contributions are incorporated in $p_i$. Encounter prefixes and marker positions have the same affine form without $j$.

Thus all these points belong to $p_i+\operatorname{span}_{\R}\{E,\nu\}$. There are finitely many $p_i$. Any finite prefix is included by adding one translate per exceptional point. This proves the common subspace assertion, including exact finite translates rather than only a tube estimate.

\begin{proposition}[Segment confinement and pointed cone]\label{smc:og:prop:cone}
Every support in cycle $n$ is within a fixed distance of the segment joining
\[
 L_0+nE\quad\hbox{and}\quad L_0+n(E+\Delta\nu).
\]
If $E$ and $\nu$ are independent, the eventual site set lies in a fixed width neighborhood of the pointed rational cone generated by $E$ and $E+\Delta\nu$. Its opening in their plane is strictly less than $180$ degrees.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A successful long flight begins within bounded distance of one marker and terminates within bounded distance of the other. Its period samples progress on the common line by a fixed nonzero multiple of $\nu$. The retained within period offsets are bounded. These facts confine the whole flight to bounded distance from the joining segment, even if individual microscopic steps do not move monotonically. Every local encounter prefix has bounded duration and bounded relative support. Marker positions at the finitely many edges differ from the section markers by bounded vectors. These bounds are uniform in $n$.

If $E,\nu$ are independent, so are $E,E+\Delta\nu$. A real linear functional can take value one on both generators. Their positive cone is therefore pointed and lies in an open half plane of their span. If $E$ is parallel to $\nu$, the cone can degenerate to a ray or a line; only rank one is asserted there.
\end{proof}

For an independent tail, stationary units add offsets but no drift. Every genuinely mobile independent component costs at least mass two, so at most two drift vectors occur. Taking their span proves the common rank two bound. If there is no unit symbol, at most two occupied sites exist and the classification supplies the same independent alternative. For a whole translated periodic tail, all phases use one drift vector. These observations cover terminal missed flights, independent dimers, zero drift periodic objects, and stationary configurations.

\section{Semilinearity and exact distinct site counts}\label{smc:og:sec:count}
A subset of $\Z^d$ is semilinear if it is a finite union of sets
\[
 a+\NN v_1+\cdots+\NN v_s,
 \qquad a,v_i\in\Z^d.
\]
Presburger arithmetic permits integer addition, order, congruences, Boolean operations, and quantifiers, but not products of variables. Its definable sets are precisely the semilinear sets.

\subsection{Membership before enumeration}
For each phase, existentially quantify $n,j$ in its integer domain and impose equation~\eqref{smc:og:eq:phase}. Disjoin all phases, marker and encounter lists, and finite prefix points. This gives an effective Presburger formula for $y\in S$. Independent rays have the same property.

The counted free variable must be $y$. Different cycles, periods, states, or phases can describe the same site. Summing the sizes of their parameter domains would count multiplicities and can change both coefficients and growth degree. Existential site membership removes them before counting.

Woods' Theorem 1.5~\cite{woods2015} supplies the standard equivalence with rational characteristic generating functions. For signed coordinates, use a disjoint orthant partition and reflection into $\NN^d$, or the injective encoding
\[
 y\longmapsto(y_1^+,y_1^-,\ldots,y_d^+,y_d^-),
 \quad y_i^+=\max(y_i,0),\quad y_i^-=\max(-y_i,0).
\]
An unrestricted two sided Laurent sum may have incompatible expansions or no common domain of convergence. No such unspecified formal series is needed.

\subsection{Existing Presburger counting theorem}
The formula
\[
 y\in S\quad\wedge\quad -N\le y_i\le N\quad(1\le i\le d)
\]
has finite fibers in the spatial variable $y$. Woods' Theorem 1.10 gives an effective piecewise quasipolynomial count in $N$. In one nonnegative parameter, finitely many rational polyhedral pieces leave finitely many exceptional bounded intervals and eventual residue polynomials. This is eventual quasipolynomiality. With independently variable endpoints, the same theorem gives a multivariate piecewise quasipolynomial.

These are applications of existing counting results, not new general theorems about Presburger arithmetic. Their use is effective without a complexity promise. The special work here is extracting the correct finite fiber family and proving its sharp degree.

\subsection{Sharp degree and common leading coefficient}
\begin{proof}[Proof of the counting assertions in Theorem~\ref{smc:og:thm:geometry}]
A rational rank $r$ affine plane has $O(N^r)$ lattice points in $[-N,N]^d$. Choose $r$ coordinate projections independent on its direction space; on an exact affine plane these determine at most one point. Finitely many parallel translates still give $O(N^r)$. For finite width, bounded integer transverse offsets give the same estimate. Thus an expanding tail has $C(N)=O(N^r)$ for $r=\rank_\Q\{E,\nu\}$.

Fix one genuine growing flight phase, present on all sufficiently large cycles or on one fixed positive density residue class. Its period parameter has at least $an-b$ values, with $a>0$. For a sufficiently small constant $\eta>0$, the sites with $n\le\eta N$ all lie in the radius $N$ box once finite exceptions are removed.

If $r=2$, the vectors $E+\varepsilon\Delta\nu$ and $m\nu$ are independent. Hence $(n,j)\mapsto y$ is injective within this single phase. There are $\Theta(N)$ admissible cycle values and $\Theta(n)$ positions in cycle $n$, yielding $\Omega(N^2)$ distinct sites.

If $r=1$, that two parameter map need not be injective. Instead choose just one admissible cycle $n$ within a bounded distance of $\eta N$. Its $\Theta(N)$ period values give distinct sites because $m\nu\ne0$. This proves $\Omega(N)$ without intercycle injectivity.

Every eventual residue polynomial therefore has exact degree $r$. Its leading coefficient is common across residues: monotonicity $C(N)\le C(N+1)$ orders consecutive coefficients, and following the whole residue cycle forces equality. The common coefficient $\kappa$ is positive and rational. Lower order terms are uniformly $O(N^{r-1})$.

For independent and translated periodic tails, each nonconstant ray contributes $\Theta(N)$ sites, whereas constant rays contribute finitely many. This proves their degree claims and the finite set characterization of degree zero.
\end{proof}
In particular, the ordinary generating function $\sum_{N\ge0}C(N)z^N$ is rational. For some effective period $M$, after accounting for finitely many initial terms its denominator divides $(1-z^M)^{r+1}$.

\subsection{Why multibox chambers matter}
For the basic wedge $\{(n,j):n\ge0,\ 0\le j\le n\}$, the centered rectangle count equals
\[
 \begin{cases}
 (N_1+1)(N_1+2)/2,&N_1\le N_2,\\[2pt]
 (N_2+1)(N_2+2)/2+(N_1-N_2)(N_2+1),&N_1\ge N_2.
 \end{cases}
\]
The chamber boundary is real; one global multivariate quasipolynomial is not generally available. Along $N_2=0$ the degree drops to one. Translated or degenerate boxes can miss every unbounded component. A positive centered rational dilation in every coordinate preserves the sharp degree, after resolving floors by residues. A general affine family guarantees only the upper degree bound.

\section{Quadratic clocks and discovery inverses}\label{smc:og:sec:clock}
Constrain the phase witnesses by $0\le n<K$ to count all sites through complete cycles. The free variable remains $y$, so $D(K)$ is a finite fiber Presburger count. All its positions have norm $O(K)$ in the common rank $r$ subspace. The same growing phase lower bounds prove $D(K)=\Theta(K^r)$. Monotonicity again forces one positive rational leading coefficient $\alpha$ across residues, proving~\eqref{smc:og:eq:Dgeneral}.

In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{smc:og:eq:newpercycle}
 D(K+1)-D(K)=O(K^{r-1}).
\end{equation}
For rank one, an entire late cycle discovers only boundedly many new sites even though its shuttle makes $\Theta(K)$ visits along a long flight.

\subsection{Retaining the clock exactly}
For sufficiently large $T$, let $n(T)$ be the unique cycle with $T_{n(T)}\le T<T_{n(T)+1}$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{smc:og:eq:rootclock}
 n(T)=\left\lfloor\frac{-b+\sqrt{b^2+4c(T-T_0)}}{2c}\right\rfloor
       =\sqrt{T/c}+O(1).
\end{equation}
The discriminant is nonnegative in the eventual range. The increasing quadratic on nonnegative cycle indices makes the indicated root the appropriate one.

Since $D(n(T))\le V(T)\le D(n(T)+1)$, substitution in~\eqref{smc:og:eq:Dgeneral} gives~\eqref{smc:og:eq:Vgeneral}. Thus rank one has square root discovery with bounded error, and rank two has linear discovery with error $O(\sqrt T)$. Merely knowing $T_n\sim cn^2$ would prove the leading asymptotic equivalence, but the displayed error requires $T_n=cn^2+O(n)$.

There is an exact description stronger than this asymptotic. For current cycle $n$ and time offset $s$, count the distinct sites from the prefix, earlier cycles, and current phase occurrences with their affine elapsed time at most $s$. This is a Presburger finite fiber count $Q(n,s)$, hence effectively piecewise quasipolynomial. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{smc:og:eq:exactQ}
 V(T)=Q\bigl(n(T),T-T_{n(T)}\bigr).
\end{equation}
The assertion of piecewise quasipolynomiality belongs to $Q$, not to its composition with the quadratic floor root. Section boundaries and within cycle oscillations have not been discarded.

\subsection{Spatial and cycle inverses}
For an infinite spatial set define
\[
 R(M)=\min\{N:C(N)\ge M\},\qquad
 J(M)=\min\{K:D(K)\ge M\}.
\]
Comparing $\kappa N^r+O(N^{r-1})$ with the level $M$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{smc:og:eq:spatialinverse}
 R(M)=(M/\kappa)^{1/r}+O(1),\qquad
 J(M)=(M/\alpha)^{1/r}+O(1).
\end{equation}
Indeed a fixed sufficiently large shift of $(M/\kappa)^{1/r}$ changes the leading polynomial by enough to dominate the uniform $O(N^{r-1})$ error.

These inverses are also exactly effective. On each eventual residue $N=u+Pq$ for a fixed period $P$, substitute into the corresponding degree one or two polynomial for $C(N)$, solve the eventual increasing linear or quadratic inequality, and minimize over the finitely many residues and finite exceptional values. This produces finitely many rational affine ceiling formulas in rank one or quadratic root ceiling formulas in rank two. In rank one $R$ is eventually quasi affine. In rank two its square root growth rules out eventual quasipolynomiality.

For discovery of $M$ distinct sites, let
\[
 H(M)=\min\{T:V(T)\ge M\}.
\]
Monotonicity and~\eqref{smc:og:eq:Vgeneral} give
\begin{equation}\label{smc:og:eq:Hgeneral}
 H(M)=
 \begin{cases}
 (c/\alpha^2)M^2+O(M),&r=1,\\
 (c/\alpha)M+O(\sqrt M),&r=2.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
An exact rank one improvement is available because of the bounded number of new sites per cycle.

\begin{proposition}[Quadratic rank one discovery inverse]\label{smc:og:prop:Hrank1}
If $r=1$, then $H(M)$ is effectively eventually quasipolynomial of degree exactly two, with common leading coefficient $c/\alpha^2$. Accordingly $V(T)$ has an eventual exact description using a finite maximum of quadratic floor root formulas, without becoming a quasipolynomial in $T$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By~\eqref{smc:og:eq:newpercycle}, some fixed integer $B$ bounds the number of genuinely new sites in every sufficiently late cycle. The eventual graph of $D(n)$ is Presburger because $D$ is quasi affine. For each fixed $1\le\ell\le B$, ``at least $\ell$ new sites in cycle $n$ have appeared by offset $s$'' is Presburger: use $\ell$ pairwise distinct site variables, occurrence by $s$, and absence from the fixed prefix and every earlier cycle. A finite disjunction over $\ell$ defines the earliest cycle $n(M)$ and earliest offset $s(M)$ at which the count reaches $M$. This also handles cycles that introduce no new site.

These are one parameter Presburger functions, hence eventually quasi affine. An elementary proof of the underlying piecewise affine fact appears in Lemma~\ref{smc:og:lem:affine}. Substitution in
\[
 H(M)=T_0+cn(M)^2+bn(M)+s(M)
\]
